# EstreGenesis — AGENTS.md-First AI Native Seed System

**Language**: **English** (below) · [한국어](#korean)

> **Run Claude, Cursor, Copilot, Gemini, and other AI coding agents on the same codebase without rule-file chaos.** AGENTS.md-first seed prompts that bootstrap or migrate an AI-native project into a service-agnostic operating system for multi-agent collaboration.

Copy one file into your new project. Paste it as the first message to any AI coding agent. The agent runs an **interactive bootstrap** (new project) or a **structured migration** (existing project) that leaves you with a service-agnostic operating system for AI collaboration.

## Start in 60 seconds

**On Claude Code — one command.** Install the kit plugin and let it fetch the right seed for you:

```
/plugin marketplace add SoliEstre/EstreGenesis
/plugin install estregenesis@estregenesis-plugins

/egboot     # new project      (long name: /eg-bootstrap)
/egmig      # existing project (long name: /eg-migration)
/egup       # already seeded — upgrade seed + plugins (long name: /eg-upgrade)
/egmem      # reconcile agent memory ↔ project docs (long name: /eg-memsync)
/egrich     # full arm: seed + all seven modules, wired and verified
```

The skill picks the tier, fetches the seed, installs it as `.agent/seed_prompt.md`, and runs it — no hunting for a raw URL, no copy-paste. Pick the wrong one and it routes you to the right one.

**On any other agent — the manual path (still 60 seconds).**

1. Pick one seed: **Lite** ([English](AI_Native_Project_Seed_Prompt_Lite.md) / [한국어](AI_Native_프로젝트_시드_프롬프트_Lite.md)) is the default; use **Compact** ([English](AI_Native_Project_Seed_Prompt_Compact.md) / [한국어](AI_Native_프로젝트_시드_프롬프트_Compact.md)) if you already know the pattern, or **Master** ([English](AI_Native_Project_Master_Seed_Prompt.md) / [한국어](AI_Native_프로젝트_마스터_시드_프롬프트.md)) if you want every inline template.
2. Copy it into your target project as `.agent/seed_prompt.md`.
3. Paste the same file as the first message to Claude Code, Cursor, Copilot, Gemini, Codex, Cline, Windsurf, or another coding agent.

The agent will ask the Phase 0-7 bootstrap/migration questions and generate `AGENTS.md`, `.agent/`, and the bridge files you choose.

---

## Why this exists

Most AI coding guides tell you how to talk to **one** model. Real projects don't stay monogamous — you end up with a `CLAUDE.md` next to a `.cursor/rules/` next to a stray `GEMINI.md`, and the rules start to contradict each other. When two agents edit the same file at the same time, there's no coordination layer. When a blocker takes three hours to solve, the next session has no memory of the fix.

This seed bakes in patterns tested during the intensive operation of the author's second AI Native project, run with **three concurrent AI agents** (Antigravity + GitHub Copilot + Claude Code). The focus areas:

- **Preventing repeat mistakes** — `.agent/_lessons/` records every surprising blocker with tags; next session greps before touching the area.
- **Document-layer separation** — `.agent/` (agent workspace) vs `docs/` (developer runbooks) vs `executive-docs/` (strategy) vs `dashboard/` (user-action backlog) — each with a specific audience, no mixing.
- **Repo residency boundaries** — Phase 2.5 decides whether the workspace is the source repo, a private agent-docs sidecar, a multi-project orchestration repo, or an upstream/local split before choosing the `.agent/` shape.
- **Service-agnostic bridges** — `AGENTS.md` is the single source of truth. Every AI service's rule file (CLAUDE.md, GEMINI.md, .cursor/rules/, .windsurfrules, .aider.conf.yml, …) points to it or imports it where the tool supports imports. Switch services freely, rules don't break.
- **Concurrent multi-agent safety** — `.agent/_coordination/` with STATE.md, HANDOFF.md, CHANGELOG.md prevents file-edit collisions and gives every agent a live map of who's doing what.
- **Research-driven decisions** — Significant branch points (strategy, tech selection, legal, market) trigger a Research → Report → Plan → Link loop with URL-sourced evidence and honest "source unverified" markers.

---

## Why not just `CLAUDE.md`?

The seed starts paying for itself when any of these scenarios show up:

| Scenario | Single rules file (CLAUDE.md, .cursor/rules, …) | EstreGenesis pattern |
|---|---|---|
| 2nd or 3rd AI service joins the project | Duplicate rules per service file; they drift over time | Bridge files point each tool back to `AGENTS.md` — SSoT stays single |
| Two agents edit the same file at once | No coordination layer; last write wins | `_coordination/HANDOFF.md` claim ledger + live `STATE.md` map |
| 3-hour blocker solved last week | Stuck in chat history, gone next session | `_lessons/` entry, grepped at task start |
| Project already has scattered rule files | Manual triage, no template | Migration A: audit → extract `AGENTS.md` → rewrite bridges |
| Strategic decision (stack, legal, market) | Ad-hoc chat, no audit trail | Research → Report → Plan → Link loop with URL evidence |
| Seed-bootstrapped project, new seed version released | Manual diff and apply | Migration B: numbered delta menu, additive only |

If none apply, a single rules file is fine. If one applies, EstreGenesis starts paying for itself. If two or more apply, the bridge-and-SSoT pattern stops being optional.

---

## Beyond bootstrap — seven optional modules

v1.x is the project seed — bootstrap a new AI-native project, or migrate an existing one, into the `AGENTS.md` SSoT (everything above). **v2.0+ adds seven optional modules** layered on top:

- **[Constellation](Constellation.md)** (v2.0+) — live multi-agent orchestration. Graduates coordination from the file-based `.agent/_coordination/` ledger to a real-time live board (WebSocket + A2A messaging + dashboard). The **A2A bridge interface** is the invariant contract; reference runtime under [constellation/reference/](constellation/reference/) is Node deps-0 except `ws`. Components authored as `.eux` and brewed with **[EstreUX](https://github.com/SoliEstre/EstreUX)** — a separate Apache-2.0 runtime EG references (clone-and-run), not bundles or owns.

- **[Superscalar](Superscalar.md)** (v2.3+) — execution-scheduling discipline for parallel sub-agent dispatch. Five-dimension `issue_width` formula (effort_band + pace_mode + Little's Law + Kanban WIP + autonomy_available_workers) + cost-benefit-gated dispatch at the 30–60k token-horizon crossover + worktree-isolated reorder buffer + in-order retire + consistency gate + opt-in speculation. Production-ready (Stage 1 ships). n=8 dogfood ledger + Entry 06 controlled A/B showing "parallelism is not sufficient; orchestration discipline produces consistency."

- **[Hyperbrief](Hyperbrief.md)** (v2.3.20+) — decision-delegation gating discipline. Every user-facing decision question runs through a trigger rubric; if it escalates, the LLM emits a schema-enforced JSON IR and a deterministic Node renderer produces a 9-section Markdown brief + interactive HTML card. 4-axis × 5-level tone profile + skill-side auto-localize (button label + MD trigger phrases follow the user's prevailing conversation language). Cross-module integrations active: Constellation §13.16.9 (5 A2A intent names + ack_tier='decided') + Superscalar §3.1 (orthogonal gate at write/deploy/send lane retire). §11.5 v1.0 readiness rubric (Lens A 7-dim module-wide GA / Lens B 6-dim host-specific marketplace registration) shipped.

- **[Greatpractice](Greatpractice.md)** (v2.5.50+, v2.5.55 release-cadence ratified) — memory-triggered practice-codification discipline. Targets the &quot;quiet omission&quot; failure surface: small obligations that should accompany the work — &quot;update the docs alongside the code,&quot; &quot;check before sending,&quot; &quot;when X changes, also change Y&quot; — that usually start as memory notes and gradually slip through as those notes accumulate. Greatpractice treats agent-workspace memory feedback (`memory/feedback_*.md` or equivalent) as the input trigger and routes raw signals through a 5-axis multi-criteria maturation gate (frequency + depth + recency + cost + predictability weighted sum, threshold ≥ 18 OR 3-criterion notability) into a 3-tier macro/mezzo/micro hierarchy. Ratified entries are enforced via deterministic lifecycle hooks (SessionStart blocking / UserPromptSubmit path-scoped inject / PreToolUse 3-subtype poka-yoke contact/fixed-value/motion-step / PostToolUse SSoT propagation / Stop cycle-end probe / PostCompact working-set restore). Explicit `phronesis_boundary` field carves out judgement-heavy work that should NOT be codified. Backed by 9-axis cross-domain deep research (harness · humanities · psychology · management · processor · os · sre · memoization · canonical). v2.5.55 dual cut promotes the [`greatpractice/macro/release-cadence.md`](greatpractice/macro/release-cadence.md) entry from `_propose/draft` to ratified (user steering trigger at N=1 evidence; mezzo decomposition 8-candidate batch scheduled v2.5.56+).

- **[Ultrasafe](Ultrasafe.md)** (v2.5.55+) — pre-release / pre-update simulated penetration testing discipline. Targets the &quot;ship-then-discover&quot; failure surface: security problems found by external users after release rather than by internal verification before. Ultrasafe ships every release through a Superscalar-applied Workflow where 8 parallel red-team agents (v0.1.0 minimum fan-out — AI/LLM · Web/API · Supply · Crypto · Social · Method/Comp · TM/Lifecycle · Synthesizer + GTA/DSP cross-cutting) simulated-attack from independent perspectives, emit findings via the Finding Output Contract (§4 of the spec), aggregate at the Synthesizer with BFT 2f+1 quorum + cross-axis correlation, and produce a 3-layer report (OSCAL Assessment Result + Hyperbrief 9-section IR + Greatpractice tree candidate). The ≥3 iteration loop runs until 4-condition AND clean signal: regression-free + monotonic improvement + coverage gate + 2 iter consecutive with agent diversity invariant. Dual pre-release trigger (PreToolUse hook on `git push --tags` / `npm publish` / `gh release` / etc. 7 matchers + `/ultrasafe` skill). 5 new Constellation A2A intents (`ULTRASAFE_FINDING` / `ITERATION_BOUNDARY` / `RELEASE_GATE` / `SECURITY_DISCLOSURE_INTAKE` / `MPCVD_COORDINATION`). **Advisory-only v0.1.x → blocking v0.2.x** staged transition (Tier 3 release strict / Tier 1/2 opt-in). Backed by 17-axis cross-domain deep research (harness · web · AI · threat-modeling · multi-agent · adversarial-ml · fuzzing · supply-chain · devsecops · IR · crypto · human-factors · game-theory · compliance · cognitive · visualization · protocol-trust-evolution). v0.1.0 first cut ships the full spec (2544 lines, §1-§13 + appendix A-C) + plugin manifest; runtime (attack agent dispatch / iteration loop runner / 3-layer report generator / 5 A2A intent handlers / PreToolUse hooks / MCP server) deferred to v0.2+ roadmap.

- **[Compendium](Compendium.md)** (v0.2.6) — a **portable discipline layer over whatever markdown vault you already use**: one definition per concept, carried in two registers (plain + expert), pointing at the spec that owns it instead of copying it. That last property is the wedge — a Compendium entry cannot drift from its source, because it never restates it. It reifies the north-star's second axis (concept/vocabulary survival over code survival — the layer most likely to outlive any single runtime implementation). Shipped: the content store, gardening lints (broken pointer / orphan / duplicate concept / stale), an 8-tool MCP server, two cross-tool skills (`compendium-curate` / `compendium-lint`) that any agent can read, and a vault projection so the store opens in Obsidian-class apps with its link graph intact.

- **[Corporate](Corporate.md)** (v0.1.x, 🌱 design draft — runtime deferred as named prunable units) — durable-role agent organization; the third orchestration axis: Superscalar decides how many lanes and at which tier, Constellation decides how agent processes talk, **Corporate decides who exists**. Its unit is the durable role — a named seat that outlives any session or process, so it can accumulate practice, be addressed across time, own units in a coordination registry, and give a decision an addressee. Ships the two-space desk contract (gitignored private desk / tracked public locker), four residency classes defaulting to the cheapest, a three-layer toggle chain read by exactly one resolver, and two declaration events (CorporateChart / RoleState) that let a board render an organization it hosts none of.

All seven modules are **optional** and **referenced** (not bundled into the seed tiers, so the tier seeds stay lean). File-based coordination (Phase 5) remains the default and is enough for most projects.

### Install as Claude Code plugins (self-hosted marketplace)

The same seven modules also ship as Claude Code plugins via a **self-hosted marketplace** in this repo. From any Claude Code session:

```
/plugin marketplace add SoliEstre/EstreGenesis
/plugin install estregenesis@estregenesis-plugins
/plugin install constellation@estregenesis-plugins
/plugin install superscalar@estregenesis-plugins
/plugin install hyperbrief@estregenesis-plugins
/plugin install greatpractice@estregenesis-plugins
/plugin install ultrasafe@estregenesis-plugins
/plugin install compendium@estregenesis-plugins
/plugin install corporate@estregenesis-plugins
```

Eight plugins: the **estregenesis** kit (the seed itself, as `/egboot` · `/egmig` · `/egup` · `/egmem` · `/egrich`) plus the seven modules. Each is independent — install one, some, or all of them. The marketplace metadata lives at [.claude-plugin/marketplace.json](.claude-plugin/marketplace.json); each plugin's source is under [plugins/](plugins/). Apache-2.0. Current: estregenesis v0.4.2 (5 procedures + 4 pointer aliases, 0 deps); Constellation v0.3.40 (+ 5-tool MCP server, 8 skills incl. `/boardsweep`, Stop hook); Superscalar v0.5.0 (+ §3.1 Hyperbrief interlock); Hyperbrief v0.9.0 (+ 3 skills, 4-tool MCP server, PreToolUse/Stop hooks); Greatpractice v0.3.5 (+ `/routinize`, 3 JSON schemas, 1 contact hook); Ultrasafe v0.2.14 (+ 8-agent red-team fan-out, clean-signal gate — advisory, never blocks a publish); Compendium v0.2.10 (+ 8-tool MCP server, gardening lint, 2 cross-tool skills); Corporate v0.1.9 (+ 4 skills incl. `/corporate-sweep`, the single toggle resolver with an executable identity test, a 4-harness invocation registry, 0 deps). Anthropic-side community-marketplace listing is deferred to v1.0 GA per [Hyperbrief.md §11.5](Hyperbrief.md) readiness rubric Lens B.

## Three tiers — pick one per project

| Tier | Size | Primary use | Target reader |
|---|---|---|---|
| **Master** | ~2460 lines | New projects that need deep guidance, teams learning the pattern for the first time, edge cases where you need every inline template (full AGENTS.md + `.gitignore` per-stack rows + escape/HTML/PDF scripts + bridge templates) | First-time AI Native author; teams formalizing a process |
| **Lite** | ~1140 lines | Quick new projects, migration sessions, onboarding new AI services into existing projects, when the master would eat too much context window. Self-contained — embeds inline templates for AGENTS.md, `.agent/rules.md`, `.gitignore`, scripts, and bridge stubs in compressed form | Returning author who remembers the pattern; most projects |
| **Compact** | ~155 lines | Authors who already know the pattern and want the minimum viable seed; tightest context window; bullet triggers + algorithm-spec descriptions only (the agent generates the actual files following the specs) | Power user who just needs a checklist |

You place **one tier** into your project. Not all three. Cross-referencing tiers that aren't present produces dead links and agent confusion, so each tier is **self-contained** — internally complete, no forward or backward references to other tiers.

When a project grows to need more detail, you don't upgrade tiers in-place. You simply replace the file with the next tier and commit. Agents treat it as a normal doc update.

## Migration scenarios

For a new project, see [Start in 60 seconds](#start-in-60-seconds) above. To migrate an existing project, pick the path below — see [§ Migration details](#migration-details-the-unique-contribution) for red flags and edge cases per scenario.

### Migration A — existing project, no formal AI collaboration setup

1. Copy the **Lite** file into the project (Lite has the best migration prose).
2. Paste as first message.
3. Tell the agent: *"This project already exists — please audit first and propose migration."*
4. Agent enters **Migration A** mode: audits every `CLAUDE.md` / `.cursor/rules/` / scattered agent notes, presents inventory, proposes extraction of service-agnostic rules into a new `AGENTS.md` SSoT.
5. Review the extraction, approve, let the agent point bridge files back to `AGENTS.md`, reorganize `.agent/`, and record the migration in `.agent/_lessons/001_AI_Native_Migration.md`.

### Migration B — existing project, already seed-bootstrapped with an older version

1. Copy the current-version seed into the project (same tier as the previous one — don't tier-upgrade during a version migration).
2. Paste as first message.
3. Tell the agent: *"Project was bootstrapped with an earlier version of this seed — please upgrade additively."*
4. Agent enters **Migration B** mode: identifies starting version, diffs capabilities (missing research loop? missing multi-agent coordination? missing migration guides themselves?), presents numbered delta menu.
5. Review, approve selected deltas, agent applies additively — never rewriting existing user customizations.

### Migration C — hybrid

Parts of the project are seed-compliant, parts are ad-hoc. Agent runs **Migration C**: subsystem-by-subsystem, applying A or B as appropriate, documenting the hybrid origin.

## What the seed produces

After Phase 7 (bootstrap) or a migration completes, the default source-repo shape is:

```
your-project/
├── AGENTS.md                          ← single source of truth for all AI services
├── .agent/
│   ├── seed_prompt.md                 ← the seed file you placed (so agents can re-read)
│   ├── rules.md                       ← detailed work rules
│   ├── architecture.md                ← tech stack, data flow, dependencies
│   ├── _coordination/                 ← multi-agent workspace (if applicable)
│   │   ├── STATE.md                   ← live "who's doing what"
│   │   ├── HANDOFF.md                 ← file-claim ledger
│   │   └── CHANGELOG.md               ← completion log
│   ├── _contracts/                    ← API/Event/Type contracts between parts
│   ├── _questions/                    ← async cross-agent Q&A
│   │   ├── open/
│   │   └── resolved/
│   ├── _lessons/                      ← troubleshooting memory
│   └── PM/                            ← plans, 3-digit prefix
│       └── 001_Phase1_Plan.md
├── CLAUDE.md                          ← Claude Code bridge (points to AGENTS.md)
├── GEMINI.md                          ← Gemini bridge
├── .github/copilot-instructions.md    ← Copilot bridge
├── .cursor/rules/main.mdc             ← Cursor bridge
├── (other bridges based on your Phase 4 choices)
├── docs/                              ← developer runbooks (optional)
├── executive-docs/                    ← strategy, legal (optional, larger projects)
├── dashboard/                         ← user-action backlog (optional)
└── meetings/                          ← meeting records (optional)
```

Phase 2.5 may choose a non-default `<scope-root>` first: direct `.agent/` for ordinary source repos, `.agent/<unit-project-name>/` for multi-project orchestration, or `<scope-root>/project/` plus `<scope-root>/upstream/` for upstream-bound work. Sidecar setups add source-map/public-boundary docs and only add a source folder to `.gitignore` after the user identifies it and the agent verifies it exists in the workspace.

Every AI service that joins the project later reads `AGENTS.md` and is immediately productive. No service-specific onboarding, no "wait, what are the rules here?" — everything lives in one place.

---

## The operating loop (after bootstrap)

Every agent — the one you use daily, and any new one joining — follows this 8-step loop:

The list below uses default `.agent/` paths; replace them with `<scope-root>` when Phase 2.5 selected a non-default residency shape.

1. Read `AGENTS.md` (SSoT)
2. Read `.agent/rules.md` + `.agent/architecture.md`
3. Read `.agent/_coordination/STATE.md` (multi-agent projects only)
4. Grep `.agent/_lessons/` for tags related to the current task
5. Claim shared files in `.agent/_coordination/HANDOFF.md` before editing
6. Record blockers in `.agent/_questions/open/`
7. Log completion in `.agent/_coordination/CHANGELOG.md`, remove from STATE.md
8. Record surprises (>30 min investigations) in `.agent/_lessons/`

This is the OS of an AI-native project. Steps 4, 7, 8 are the memory. Steps 3, 5, 6 are the coordination. Steps 1, 2 are the constitution.

## Supported AI services (bridges scaffolded out of the box)

| Service | Bridge file |
|---|---|
| Claude Code | `CLAUDE.md` + `.claude/rules/` |
| Google Antigravity / Gemini CLI | `GEMINI.md` |
| GitHub Copilot (VS Code agent) | `.github/copilot-instructions.md` |
| Cursor | `.cursor/rules/main.mdc` |
| Windsurf | `.windsurfrules` |
| Aider | `.aider.conf.yml` + read list |
| Continue.dev | `.continue/config.yaml` |
| Cline | `.clinerules/main.md` |
| Amazon Q Developer | `.amazonq/rules/main.md` |
| Zed / generic | `.rules` |
| OpenAI Codex CLI | Full adapter included (`codex/`): `node codex/gen-codex-adapter.cjs --install` projects all plugin skills into Codex's discovery path, `config.toml.example` registers the 4 MCP servers, and `codex/AGENTS.md` maps each lifecycle hook to a documented manual routine |
| Jules / Kiro / Kimi Code / others | Read `AGENTS.md` directly (Kimi Code treats `AGENTS.md` as its first-class instruction file — its own `/init` generates one) |

Every bridge points the service back to `AGENTS.md` (or imports it where supported) plus whatever service-specific knobs that tool requires. Switching services is a no-op — the SSoT stays.

When a new AI service launches, you just add one more bridge file. The rule system doesn't have to change.

---

## Migration details (the unique contribution)

Bootstrap is useful. Migration is where this seed earns its keep. Three scenarios covered in every tier:

### Migration A — scattered AI files → `AGENTS.md` standard

Your project already has `CLAUDE.md` with 143 lines of rules, a `.cursor/rules/` with 89 lines, some `.agent/notes/decisions.md` that never got organized, and no single source of truth. The agent audits the inventory first (never scaffolds blind), extracts service-agnostic rules into a fresh `AGENTS.md`, rewrites each existing bridge file to import from it, reorganizes `.agent/` into the standard shape, and records what moved where in `.agent/_lessons/001_AI_Native_Migration.md`. Red flags — conflicting rules across bridges, prior git history in `.agent/`, custom coordination schemes the user prefers to keep — stop the agent and require explicit user decision before proceeding.

### Migration B — previous seed version → current

Project was bootstrapped with an older version of this seed (missing the research loop, multi-agent coordination layer, migration guides, or Bootstrap Residency checks). The agent identifies the starting version, diffs capabilities, presents a numbered menu of available deltas. The user picks which to apply. Every addition is marked with `<!-- added in seed vX.Y migration, YYYY-MM-DD -->` so future readers can trace lineage. **Existing user customizations are preserved** — the agent never re-runs the Phase 0-7 interview on a migrated project.

### Migration C — hybrid

Parts of the project are seed-compliant, parts are ad-hoc custom structure. The agent runs A on the custom parts, B on the seed-compliant parts, merges results in `AGENTS.md`, and documents the hybrid origin. Hybrid migrations often surface real business logic that a pure A or B would miss — the seed treats this as a high-value lessons-file opportunity, not a nuisance.

---

## Design principles

The seed reflects six opinions earned the hard way:

1. **Docs before code, not the other way around.** Every decision lives in a file. An agent that can't find the decision will reinvent (often differently from last time).
2. **One SSoT (`AGENTS.md`), many bridges.** Service proliferation is a given. Centralize shared rules, let each service keep its own knobs.
3. **Memory compounds through `_lessons/`.** A 30-minute debug session becomes a 30-second grep next time.
4. **Coordination is explicit, not implicit.** Multi-agent projects need STATE/HANDOFF/CHANGELOG. "Just trust each other not to collide" does not scale past two agents.
5. **Research-driven decisions for branch points.** Ad-hoc strategic decisions are how projects compound tech debt silently. Research → Report → Plan → Link turns every major choice into an auditable trail.
6. **Repo residency before doc shape.** Public/collab source repos, private agent-docs repos, multi-project orchestration, and upstream-bound work need different `<scope-root>` decisions before files are created.

---

## Versioning

Each tier has its own version. Master is the authoritative evolution track; Lite and Compact are derived regularly from Master but may lag by a release.

**Current**: v2.6.100 (2026-08-09) — the terminal relay's session scoping closes §9's third door properly: terminal bytes now reach only the operator who opened the session, enforced by the server, not merely filtered by the widget. Before this, `TerminalData` broadcast to every board and the widget dropped what wasn't its session id — but the bytes still crossed the wire to every connected dashboard, visible in any of their devtools; a render filter is "not shown," not "not received." The server now records the board connection that sent each `PtyOpen` and routes that session's `TerminalData`/`TerminalExit` to that connection alone, and the `PtyData`/`PtyResize`/`PtyClose` keystroke frames travel operator→pty-host only, excluded from the other-board broadcast and from history because a shell keystroke is private. When an owner board disconnects, its sessions are cleaned up and a `PtyClose` goes to pty-host so the orphaned shell does not leak. Verified with two board clients: the owner receives the echo, the eavesdropper receives zero frames for the session. What remains: the direct↔relay mode switch on seat-CLI entry (T2 proper), reauth-at-entry wiring, and pty-host auto-start under the watchdog.

Previously: v2.6.94 (2026-08-08) — the board showed a context figure that could not be traced to a model. The cause was not a missing label but a moving source: seat telemetry picked the most recently modified transcript inside one project folder and published it under a fixed seat name, and that folder holds several sessions, so the figure switched between models silently while the name above it stayed the same. Adding a label to that would have produced a confidently wrong label, which is worse than none. Meanwhile a second declared seat had no telemetry at all — not unknown, absent — because only one project folder was ever read, and absence and "has not run yet" render identically. Binding is now derived per seat from the session id the seat's own supervisor already records, so it is exact where an exact answer exists, and the binding method travels with the value (session-file, mtime, or none) because an approximation rendered like a measurement reintroduces the same misreading after the source is fixed. The means-ladder rung travels too: its absence had made a three-day parking on the fallback engine indistinguishable from normal operation, and before that a six-hour ladder oscillation — fifteen thousand rung changes at a median of eighty-eight milliseconds, driven by a fallback that could not start at all — presented as nothing rather than as an error. Background work now carries the model each lane runs on, derived from that lane's own transcript; the monitor had only stat'd those files and never opened them, so the model was not withheld, it had never been read. A lookup that fails stays null and renders as unknown rather than being filled in, because the correspondence between a workflow's agent identifier and its transcript filename could not be measured at publication time and a plausible wrong label is believed while a missing one is questioned. The seat shown on the input line now follows the seat being addressed instead of whichever one sorts first, so the number on screen and the recipient of the next message are the same seat by construction.

Previously: v2.6.93 (2026-08-08) — the operator asked why selecting the local group addressed a channel belonging to an upstream peer. Two functions were answering the same question — who belongs to this group — and answering it differently: the tab renderer excluded hidden channels and applied the operator's saved tab order, while the send-target resolver did neither and took the first member in map-insertion order. The leftmost tab on screen and the channel a message would actually reach could therefore diverge, and a hidden channel could become the group representative, which means addressing a channel that is not on screen at all. The resolver now reads the rendered group, so the representative is the first visible tab by construction rather than by coincidence, and all seven groups were measured to agree afterwards. Nothing had left the board — the upstream channels showed no traffic in the preceding six hours, so a wrong indicator was caught before it became a wrong send, which is the only reason this is a display defect and not a disclosure. One thing was deliberately left alone: an unrecognised role still falls back to local, and the line that does it now says so. Removing that fallback would drop those channels out of every group and off the screen, and absence is a worse failure than miscategorisation, because a channel nobody can see is a channel nobody can correct. The mitigation is that the representative is now what the operator sees, so a miscategorised channel is visible in the place it was wrongly filed rather than silently selected behind the interface.

Previously: v2.6.91 (2026-08-07) — Two credential-boundary corrections in the board runtime, found by an automated review two days before this runtime first ships publicly. The operator credential file, which holds password hashes, was written with default permissions and chmodded afterwards — a window during which another user on the same host could read it, and one that `mode` alone does not close because it applies only when a file is created, leaving an already-loose file loose on overwrite. It is now written to a temporary file created at 0600 and moved into place atomically, hardened again after the move, and hardened on load so a file that predates this cut is rescued rather than trusted. And loopback is demoted from an identity to a defense layer: the host-local predicate correctly rejects proxied requests, but what remains after that rejection is *someone on this host* rather than a known operator — and one of the endpoints it guarded rewrites the bind address, so a local unprivileged process could widen the board to every interface. Both mutating admin endpoints now require a loopback origin **and** an authenticated operator session, while the additive contract is preserved exactly: with zero accounts the layer does not exist and cannot be required, so the check falls back to loopback and, when the board is exposed in that state, says so on every request rather than passing silently. The third loopback call site is deliberately unchanged and now says why in place — it serves local workers, which are agents rather than operators, and its threat model is remote reconnaissance, which the loopback test does close. Naming the two that changed without naming the one that did not is how a deliberate scope reads as an omission to the next reader.

Previously: v2.6.90 (2026-08-07) — Constellation v2.4.149 · Pantty v0.1.1 — the seat-driving reference runtime moves out of the Constellation tree to the module root, completing the promotion the previous cut began: `constellation/reference/runtime/pantty/` becomes `pantty/`, alongside the other modules' own asset directories. The move was safe because nothing imports those files — a sweep found no require, no import and no spawn of them from any consumer; they are standalone scripts that compute their own workspace paths, and the only machine coupling in the whole repository was a single path constant inside one checker. That makes the location a pure ownership signal rather than a dependency, and ownership was settled when the module was promoted. History entries in the changelog and in the README version chain deliberately keep the old path, because they record what a given version said rather than where the files are now — correcting them would falsify the record. Two guards earned their keep on the way: the retarget script counts anchors before writing and refused a file where the bilingual attributes made seven occurrences of a path where three were expected, and the docs-meta axis caught a hero badge bumped to the new version while the page's own description still summarised the previous one. And the move surfaced something the old layout had been hiding — compiled Python bytecode tracked in the public repository, machine-specific and read by nobody, now untracked and excluded going forward. Touching a path is the right moment to evict what was riding along in it.

Previously: v2.6.89 (2026-08-07) — Pantty is registered as the eighth module — docs page, module card, and the version checker taught to count both questions instead of one. The checker had three hand-written module lists and a fourth derivation coupled to plugins, while a comment one axis away said enumeration drifts in both directions; promoting an eighth module bit all four at once, so the fix was to stop needing a list rather than to append to it. Modules are now derived from root specs declaring `module:` in frontmatter, which also removes an unstated requirement that a module ship a plugin — Pantty is specification-stage and an empty plugin would be intake with no value. The first attempt unified all four onto one derivation and the checker immediately said that was too much: the seed registry asks which modules an adopter can *install* and requires a plugin path, while the docs and README counts ask how many specifications exist. Two axes, two questions, one denominator each — the same shape as measuring a region's plan availability against a VM-only listing and finding the difference is a bare-metal machine. A third hand list was found in the distro composer, where module specs were matched by an alternation of seven names; that one is now matched by shape (`^[A-Z][a-z]+\.md$` catches every module spec and no other root file) rather than by frontmatter, because the composer reads committed refs and a working-tree derivation would disagree with itself when composing an old tag. Its coverage assertion is what surfaced the gap: composition refused to run rather than silently dropping the new file. And the docs-meta axis was reporting a count it had not verified — a module without a docs page was skipped by a bare `continue`, so the line read "8 modules checked" while seven were; absence is now named in the output and not folded into the pass.

Previously: v2.6.88 (2026-08-06) — Constellation v2.4.148 — §13.35 resident seats is promoted out of this specification into its own module, `Pantty.md`, and what remains here is a pointer table plus the board side of the relationship. The move is a relocation rather than a revision: every requirement carries over with the same force, with all fifty MUST-class statements and all eight MUST NOTs accounted for, and the reference runtime under `constellation/reference/runtime/pantty/` is untouched. The subsection numbers stay, because §13.35.8 is cited from §13.38.5 and §13.39.2 and adopters hold citations this specification cannot enumerate — renumbering would break them silently, which is the exact failure §13.39.2 refuses when it declines to relocate the instances it names. A specification that promotes a section owes its readers the old address and not only the new one, so each module section closes with a marker naming its former Constellation number and the pair is auditable in both directions rather than only from here. A new §13.35.10 states what deliberately does not move: a seat remains a participant under §3.1 for keys and §13.25.11 for declaration binding, the supervisor's wall-clock ceiling and the completion-by-state rule remain §13.27, telemetry reaches the board as a change-triggered declaration under §13.23.4, and recovery failures surface through §13.20. Pantty specifies what a seat must be; this specification continues to specify how a board speaks to one. Registering the new module across the docs pages, the seed registry and the version checker is deliberately not part of this cut — which is why the consistency checker reports clean here: the module is invisible to it rather than wired into it, and that distinction is recorded rather than left for a later reader to mistake for completion.

Previously: v2.6.87 (2026-08-06) — Constellation v2.4.147 — the board's current-work band folds, per item and all at once. Three concurrent top-level items already consume the whole viewport, so reaching the planned queue below meant scrolling past everything in flight on every visit. A chevron on each card title collapses that card to its title; one on the band header collapses or expands the whole band, with its state derived from the cards rather than from a flag of its own so the two controls cannot disagree. The fold key is derived from item content rather than position, because an index key silently reassigns a collapsed state to a different card as soon as a finished item leaves the band. The collapsed rule hides the complement of the title rather than an enumeration of children, so an element added later cannot quietly stop folding. The load-bearing part is neither: the planned section's sticky header is positioned from a measured custom property holding the band height, so folding without re-measuring leaves the page looking folded while everything below stays pushed down by the old height — no error, and invisible in a screenshot. The browser assertion therefore checks that the height changed rather than that the class toggled, because the class alone passes on CSS while the height moves only if the layout hook actually ran; measured 612 → 421 → 195 → 612px across per-item fold, fold-all and expand-all, 22/22 checks passing.

Previously: v2.6.86 (2026-08-06) — Constellation v2.4.146 §13.40 — a gate's activation condition must not derive from what the gate watches. §13.38.2 named the gate that cannot pass under correct operation; this is its mirror, and the harder of the two to find, because a gate switched on by a property of the thing it inspects is off precisely on the inputs it was built for while every other run still looks correct. The shape: a redaction gate reads a denylist that is deliberately never committed, since the list's contents are themselves the disclosure being prevented — so conditioning strictness on the list being present disables the check on exactly the accident it exists to catch, and it reports pass while doing so. Generally, the inputs on which such a gate is inactive are by construction the inputs on which its subject is unavailable, making its coverage the exact complement of its purpose. Three consequences are normative: absence is a third outcome distinct from pass and fail, and a failure under an explicit strict mode, because success on an absent subject reports having checked in place of having skipped; the activation signal must be a property of where the check runs rather than of what it checks, disqualified whenever the accident guarded against could also change it; and verification requires producing the condition — remove the subject, confirm the distinct skip and its strict failure, then separately confirm a present clean subject still passes, since without that second half an always-failing gate is indistinguishable from correct enforcement. Grounded on an independent implementation of the same contract, which audited its own redaction gate against the norm and found it returning success both with the denylist absent and with the compiled pattern set empty, corrected it to a distinct skip plus strict mode, and covered it with six tests; that implementation also records writing the conditional-strict wiring first and discarding it before shipping, which is why this ships as a norm rather than an implementation note. A second instance of the §13.38.4 pattern — one project's defect, named at contract level instead of fixed locally, locating the same defect in another codebase that had already passed review.

Previously: v2.6.85 (2026-08-05) — Constellation v2.4.145 — §13.39 requires declared and effective state to be published together for observable modes with automatic fallback, demotion, or recovery; it preserves §13.35.8 and §13.38.2 at their already-cited numbers and identifies them as instances of the same norm, and it requires an inventory of outputs the system already writes before classification because “unobservable” can be a confession that existing evidence has not yet been sought.

Previously: v2.6.84 (2026-08-03) — Constellation v2.4.144 — §13.38.4 the dual-write predicate gains its measured grounding, contributed by an independent implementation of the same contract, and the measurement establishes what a single-repository derivation could not: **the defect is at the specification layer, not the implementation layer.** Both projects independently prescribed count equality — four sites here (the guide's contract body, its promotion checklist, its drift trigger, the module manifest's mode-B guard) and the counterpart's own backend RRP — so neither implementation invented the wrong predicate; each was told to use it. That is precisely why this is stated as a norm rather than left to implementer judgement: an empty spec slot gives the next implementer a chance to stop and ask, and a wrong prescription removes even that. Measured cost of the wrong predicate: 26 consecutive failing boots; a raw gap of 1,519 events (1,246 after normalizing the retention cap) against zero actual loss; residual repair converging to 30, then 6, then 3 and stopping, where replacing the question took it to 0. §13.38.2 additionally names the consequence that measurement surfaced and reasoning had missed: a gate whose failure action is auto-revert does not merely lose its signal, it **silently un-does the operator's configuration** — that deployment was configured for dual-write and ran single-store for the entire period, the setting still reading `dual`, with no error anywhere, so declared state and running state diverged while presenting as normal operation. A deployment must therefore verify that a configured mode is the mode actually running rather than inferring it from the setting. After the correction, the first boot passed while holding 1,323 surplus rows in the shadow store and dual-write ran for the first time — a gate that had never once passed passing while holding more surplus than the discrepancy that had been failing it, which is the clearest available demonstration that the predicate separates retention from loss.

Previously: v2.6.83 (2026-08-03) — The redaction rule had no instrument for the shape it was actually violated in. §5.7 requires zero verbatim conversation quotation in the public repository, and the checker introduced for it reads vocabulary — so fourteen verbatim quotations sat in public files for two months and passed every run: eleven in the Hyperbrief dogfood entries, three more in the DB P2 reference docs (one in prose, two in version-history footers, all three the same quotation carrying an explicit decision-source attribution). A rule keeps its shape only as long as something measures it, and this one had a measurement aimed one axis away from the breach. The quotations are restated as technical inputs in place, preserving each entry's input → spec-change chain, which was the evidentiary point of the dogfood record. The checker gains a line-shaped axis to close the gap, and its design is the load-bearing part: **quoted Korean is not the signal** — the changelog carries 476 such spans, the README 159, and the specs use quotes for terminology throughout, so an axis keyed on that would have produced hundreds of false positives and been ignored within a week. The discriminating shape is blockquote + quotation mark + conversational Korean, which resolves to three occurrences repository-wide, all legitimate: two seed example prompts an adopter types and one string a skill emits. Those carry an inline `redaction-ok:` waiver whose reason prints on every run, so exceptions live at the site rather than in a list that goes quiet. An English attribution pattern is added under the same measure-first discipline: a first draft keyed on `operator's` matched sixteen occurrences of the seed's own normative tier clause (raising the harness tier is the operator's call), so the pattern was narrowed to phrasings that point at a past utterance rather than at the role, leaving the role vocabulary untouched. Five of the fifteen mutation fixtures assert the false-positive boundaries rather than the detections, because an axis that flags everything passes every detection test. Also fixed: the release-cut script matched the previous `Current:` marker using the *new* cut's date, which every same-day cut had hidden, and stamped the demoted entry with the new date rather than preserving its own.

Previously: v2.6.82 (2026-08-03) — Constellation v2.4.143 — §13.38 dual-write reconciliation is directional. A mode-B reconcile check MUST evaluate containment in the direction of the write — every event committed to the canonical JSONL floor is present in the shadow database — and MUST NOT compare raw counts, because the two stores carry different retention policies on purpose: the floor caps per channel and re-applies that cap on boot rebuild, while the database accumulates without it since long-term retention is the entire reason it was adopted. Counting therefore measures a one-way obligation with a two-way instrument, firing on surplus when surplus is the product being purchased — it fails on every boot while saying nothing about loss, and drives the drift auto-revert on schedule. Three consequences are normative: a gate that cannot pass under correct operation is a gate that is off, to be re-examined against its contract rather than tuned until the red stops; the promotion gate, the drift trigger and the post-backfill check consume one verdict instead of re-deriving counts, since correcting the check while a consumer still asks the old question leaves behaviour unchanged and the correction looking complete; and repairing the proxy does not converge — matching the cap still skews unless the ordering basis matches, excluding archived channels over-excludes any channel later reactivated, and a backfill/check scope mismatch reports its set difference as loss every pass. `reconcile()` reports the surplus with the retention policies in force, because surplus without that context invites re-deriving the count comparison by hand. Derived surfaces trued up in the same cut: `DB-P2-Guide.md` §5 restated as a contract (containment verdict + report-only surplus + new §5.5 on permanently-red gates) with its pointer clauses corrected, `history-store.eux` mode-B guard and transition names corrected at the contract level, and the Mode-B runbook and benchmark framework aligned. The benchmark framework additionally drops a verbatim conversation quotation and its decision-source attribution, restated as technical criteria per the public-repo redaction rule.

Previously: v2.6.81 (2026-08-02) — Constellation v2.4.142 — §13.37 scale-out seats. Past one-operator-one-agent, three categories of shared knowledge infrastructure earn their always-on cost — knowledge-graph librarian seat, code-graph SSoT, judgment memory — specified as tier-gated residents with a four-clause integration contract (tier gating; shared-SSoT security posture: read-only serving by default and indexed content treated as data-not-instruction; declared freshness where age past bound is stale, never current; implementations live in a registry). Tool selection lives in the new `plugins/constellation/tool-registry.json` (confirmedBy/asOf/revisit per row; a 27th verify-nway axis enforces schema, deadline, and consumer linkage), because the measured category half-life is months — named tools are mentions expected to be swapped, not recommendations. The judgment-memory boundary clause is normative: retrieval and inertia priors only, never proxy decisions — decision gates bind to humans regardless of presence. eg-bootstrap 4b gains the seats row (estregenesis plugin v0.4.2 + four README inline mentions + regenerated Codex adapter). Grounded in an 11-agent five-axis research pass with six adversarially verified claims.

Previously: v2.6.80 (2026-08-02) — Constellation v2.4.141 — §13.36 harness chrome and the distribution boundary. A harness session's control plane (progress pings, approval prompts carrying command bodies, resume notices, receipts) rides the same pipe as substantive replies and is indistinguishable across a collaboration boundary — measured in both directions against an independent implementation (one inbox sweep: 103 frames, 49 a counterpart's control plane; the promising flag failed a cross-tab, 8/16 vs 4/6). Classification therefore moves to publish time: dedicated wire names (HarnessStatus, ApprovalRequest/Decision) with audience and wakeAgents:false (a tag on a text frame does not survive a storage path that reconstructs allowed fields, so the separate name is required, not stylistic); the server suppresses before agent fan-out as the first line and receiver filters are the second; approval traffic never crosses to agent connections and an unroutable approval gets an explicit reject-or-waiting status, never a silent drop; the interim for deployments without selective relay is rate-limit-never-drop (hold per window, wake once with the held count, flush on timer and stream end).

Previously: v2.6.79 (2026-08-02) — Constellation v2.4.140 — History send guard now derives from the payload. The hand-enumerated check lagged the v2.4.71 `opsStates` / v2.4.76 `capManifests` additions, silently dropping History on newborn servers carrying only ops/capability declarations — the very class v2.4.90 was cut to prevent, recurring inside the guard itself. Surfaced by an independent implementation's parity port auditing back into the original; fixed by deriving send-worthiness from the payload rather than adding the two missing keys, so the next payload key needs no guard edit (asserted across 9 cases).

Previously: v2.6.78 (2026-08-02) — **An error that fires on every message says nothing about any of them (Constellation v2.4.139, §13.13.5)** — §13.13.2(g) already named the owner of the durable-append commitment, and that parenthetical did real work: an independent implementation read it and correctly concluded its plugin layer must not claim the tier. What the section never said was what to do when **no layer owns what a tier asserts**, and the silence produced failures on both sides of it. The claim rule is now stated generally — a layer may emit a tier only if it owns the state that tier asserts, because a layer reporting a fact it cannot know is indistinguishable from the real thing at the receiving end. Where nothing owns a durable append, neither available default is right: emitting `delivered-persist` anyway is a claim about surviving a restart that nobody can honour, and staying silent leaves the sender's pending entry open until the ladder exhausts and reports the route unreachable. The answer is to emit the tier you *do* own — commitment at the moment the agent **begins** engaging, not at completion, since an ack that waits for completion times out on exactly the long turns that most need the entry cleared. The window where a frame has arrived but a turn is still running gets no tier at all, deliberately: it is neither durably appended nor surfaced-and-engaged, so redelivery there is correct behaviour rather than a defect, and closing it requires some layer to actually take ownership of a queue append rather than an adjustment to ack timing. And dedup is restated where implementations forget it — dedup means do not apply the side effects twice, never do not acknowledge; suppressing the ack along with the effects makes a successful redelivery indistinguishable from a lost one. The reading rule on the sender's side came out of measurement rather than reasoning. `commitment-ack-absent` reports that a pending entry expired without the tier arriving; it does not report that the frame failed to arrive. On one live route every targeted envelope sent over several weeks — five distinct identifiers — ended in that error, including ones whose application layer demonstrably received, processed and replied to them, because the peer never emitted any commitment-tier ack at all. The error was a constant on that route, and a constant carries no information. So a sender checks whether the route has *ever* produced the ack before treating its absence as evidence; neither `sent` nor `Ack{delivered}` closes the question either, since in the measured case the peer acknowledged delivery one millisecond after emit while the sender declared the route unreachable ninety-eight seconds later; and a resend must carry a **new** identifier, because the dedup watermark is keyed on the old one and re-sending it reaches a receiver that correctly treats it as already-handled. The incident's most transferable part is its shape: three parties each held a true observation — no application response, work completed, zero matching records in board history — and the whole was still wrong, because no layer can see another layer's ledger. Where the ledgers disagree the only common reference is application-layer state measured on the recipient side.

Previously: v2.6.77 (2026-08-02) — **An advisory lane and a review lane open identically and differ only at the merge (Superscalar v0.13.0, §1.5.1)** — the topology already named three reconvergence modes — synthesis, retire, review — and the third one *gates*: a reviewer's block stops the producer's retire. There was no vocabulary for the thing an operator asks for far more often, which is a second reading rather than a second judgment: send this to a model from another lineage and tell me what it sees, but the call stays mine. Expressed as Producer-Reviewer it silently acquires a gate; expressed as nothing it stays an informal habit that leaves no record of what was heard and overruled. **counsel** is the fourth reconvergence mode and **Principal-Advisor** the pattern it composes into — structurally Producer-Reviewer with the gate removed, which is the entire content of the distinction. Because the two shapes are indistinguishable at dispatch, the mode is **declared in the request**, and each default failure is wrong in its own direction: counsel read as review silently adds a gate nobody approved and hands it to a lane that was never asked to judge, usually on partial input, while review read as counsel silently removes a gate the reviewer meant as binding. Both are invisible until something has already committed. So the merge records which contract it was, and the contract fixes what silence means — for a reviewer, not-yet-reviewed and therefore no retire; for an advisor, nothing at all, because absence of counsel is not consent. Disagreement may be overruled, with the reason written down. The non-gating property is what makes the pattern cheap enough to reach for: a lane that cannot block does not need the review-throughput accounting Little's Law imposes on Producer-Reviewer, and it can be dispatched on a question that is not yet fully formed — but the moment an advisory return starts gating retires, the pattern has become Producer-Reviewer and inherits every constraint it was chosen to avoid. Different lineage is structural rather than a preference, for the reason adversarial verification already routes away from the author's family: a second reading that agrees for the same reasons is not a second reading. Which model advises well on what is a separate, empirical question this specification deliberately does not answer — it belongs to the model registry's harness profile under the evidence contract kept there, where every advisory row is currently unconfirmed or refuted, and shipping the mechanism with the routing unsettled is the honest state rather than a gap. The same measurement pass that refuted one of those rows also produced the day's other registry work: vendor primary documentation confirms that the two flagship models want *opposite* verification scaffolding — one instructs removing verification prompts and never using subagents to check the model's own work, the other instructs making self-verification explicit and reports that fresh-context verifier subagents outperform self-critique. A per-model profile stops being a hypothesis at that point.

Previously: v2.6.76 (2026-08-02) — **The registry said modules were opt-in, and nothing ever measured it (harness tier, seed v2.7.0)** — since v2.5.0 the module registry has read «a menu, not a checklist; adopt only when the trigger fires», and no mechanism has ever checked. A discipline disappears when the tool enforcing it does, even while its sentence stays on the page — and this one never had a tool at all, so every bootstrap landed wherever that day's judgement put it, with no vocabulary for stating how much was on. The **harness tier** is that sentence's mechanism: `seed` < `lean` < `active` < `resident`, where the axis is neither size nor capability but **how much runs when nobody asked**. It is deliberately *not* a fifth Phase 0 question — Phase 0 already asks four, and adding a question in order to promote leanness refutes itself — so the tier is **derived and declared in one line**, defaulting to `lean`. Two invariants carry the weight. A tier rises on **need, not size**: not because a project is large but because irreversible decisions are actually present, which is what makes a decision gate earn its cost, or because a second agent actually is, which is what makes coordination earn its; raising by size means carrying layers that eat context and return nothing. And **an agent may only lower it — raising is the operator's call**, because lowering saves the operator's budget while raising spends it, the same who-pays principle that put the deep briefing tier behind an explicit request one cut earlier. The counterweight is that the floor does not move with the tier: the autonomous-execution rule and the loss / external-publish gates are tier-independent, since a norm naming only what a tier turns *off* leaves the implementer to fill in the rest from habit, and habit fills it the permissive way. `eg-bootstrap` was already making exactly this split — pure discipline mounts, anything that runs a process waits — so the tier names behaviour that was already right rather than introducing new behaviour, and every opt-in row now states which tier it would raise the project to. Writing the checker surfaced two defects in the work itself: the consumer named only three tiers, omitting `seed`, which is the state of every adopter whose host has no plugin mechanism at all — the largest group rather than an edge case; and the check's own existence test matched prose, so a changelog sentence mentioning the feature satisfied it after the entire block had been deleted. It also surfaced an older one: **all six seeds carried a delta list frozen at v2.5.0** while their headers read v2.6.1, so the upgrade menu shown to an adopter had been missing its last two releases and presented as «no such change was ever made» rather than as an error. Backfilled, and now asserted against the header on every run. Two more measurement defects fell out along the way: the README's version chain had three lines stamped with the current release while carrying three different releases' content, twelve lines across the four register copies, green for three cuts because the ordering check accepted equal versions while its own comment claimed strict — data and check both corrected, and the repair derived each true version from the changelog rather than typing three of them by hand.

Previously: v2.6.75 (2026-08-02) — **Asking for a brief meant asking for the heaviest one (Hyperbrief v0.9.0, §2.6)** — v0.8 made the *floor* a setting and left the *request* untiered, so the phrase «brief me» resolved to the nine-section pipeline and, with it, a fan-out nobody had named. The module read as heavy when what was heavy was an unrequested cost. The request now carries a tier: a bare request is a **summary**, 요약/상세/심층 map to `SUMMARY_BRIEF`/`FULL_HYPERBRIEF`/`DEEP_BRIEF`, and resolution stays `max(rubric_tier, floor, request_tier)` so a request can only ever ask for *more* — asking for a summary on an irreversible cross-module decision still yields nine sections, and AF-27 is untouched. `DEEP_BRIEF` is a new lattice top that **only an explicit request can reach**, and the reason is not caution about depth but about who chooses to pay: fan-out is the one cost in this module paid in someone else's tokens and wall-clock rather than in the model's own turn, so a tier a rubric could trigger would spend it unasked. Deep emits the same `FullBrief` IR plus an optional `verification_annex` rather than a fourth schema shape, because making the deepest tier also the most likely to fail validation is backwards, and its refutation attempts must each name a distinct lens — three verifiers given one instruction produce one opinion three times. Shipped alongside: the composition tool for the stable edition (built from a committed ref, never the working tree, with a coverage assertion that halts on any top-level entry it has no rule for), and a distribution marker so a composed copy **declares itself a copy** — measured the same day, composing into the workspace produced a second vocabulary store and the resolver correctly refused, which on screen looked only like «store not found». Excluding by name would have re-opened silently the moment someone chose a different location.

Previously: v2.6.74 (2026-08-02) — **The install paragraph wrote three plugin versions by hand, and hands lag (readme-claims axis extended)** — the marketplace already holds every plugin's version, and the install section restated three of them from memory: the kit at 0.3.2 against a real 0.4.0, Superscalar at 0.2.3 against 0.4.0, Ultrasafe at 0.2.11 against 0.2.14. Twelve stale claims across the four copies of that paragraph. What surfaced them is worth more than the fix: a composition tool built for a separate purpose generates its manifest **by deriving** module versions from the marketplace, and the moment two surfaces stated the same fact — one derived, one hand-written — the hand-written one was visibly behind. That is the general shape rather than an anecdote; the same repository has now recorded it for module counts, check-axis counts, seed markers and summary-key lists. Deriving is not merely tidier, it makes the other copy's staleness **observable**, which is the property a hand list can never have. The axis that now guards this had to solve two false-positive traps, both of which would have quietly cost trust: `Constellation v0.3.40` (the plugin) and `Constellation v2.4.137` (the module specification) are the same shape, so the comparison only runs when the MAJOR matches — plugin versions are all `0.x`, and specification mentions fall out naturally; and the `Previously` chain quotes frozen past states, where differing from today's value is correct, so those lines are exempt as a whole, on the same principle by which quoted historical counts were already exempt. The axis prints how many claims it compared, because a regex that has silently stopped matching and a paragraph that genuinely contains no claims produce the same green.

Previously: v2.6.73 (2026-08-02) — **A frame addressed to no one inherited a recipient, and the recipient was someone else's agent (Constellation v2.4.138)** — seat telemetry is emitted with no `targetAgentId` on purpose: boards receive it by broadcast, so naming a recipient would be wrong. But the server's reply-window fallback fills a missing recipient with whoever most recently addressed this side — a last resort for adapters that cannot echo an envelope — so «no one» was read as «the last speaker». Measured across the whole history: 165 telemetry frames carried a recipient the emitter never wrote, ten of them a collaborating party's agent, which woke on every heartbeat to answer «telemetry, no action». The token cost on this side was zero; on theirs it was not, and one of those replies read «Interrupting current task (iteration 1/90)» — the frames were preempting work already running there. Each reply reopened the window, so the leak was self-sustaining. The guard that should have stopped it recognised exactly one watcher's identity, because §2 said telemetry «is tagged» without saying **how**, and the implementer filled that gap with the only telemetry source that existed at the time; every stream added later was structurally un-taggable. The resolution moves the question to whoever knows the answer: **the emitter declares it**, carrying `telemetry: true`, stamped at its single outbound chokepoint rather than per call site — an observing process emits nothing but observation, so counting its frame names would leak again on the next name, the way per-surface `msgId` stamping leaked before it became a chokepoint. The classification now lives in one component the server consults, the legacy tag is still honoured so existing adopters see no change, and a small named safety net covers emitters not yet updated. The third layer is that staleness has to be visible: the server now says once — naming sender, frame and inherited recipient — whenever the reply-window fills a blank, because without that line «paired a reply» and «mailed someone else's telemetry» are the same shape on every surface that could show the problem. The checker asserts the mechanism rather than the list: a frame name nobody has enumerated is excluded on the tag alone, and a control arm confirms a genuine unaddressed reply is still paired, since killing the fallback would be a different defect wearing the same green. A mutation run on a reverted copy confirms the checker can fail — and while writing it, the checker's own anchor was found assuming a `function` declaration, so when the declaration form changed both sides missed identically and the copy-parity assertion passed on two empty strings. §2 now states how tagging is set and that the inherited peer may sit outside this deployment's cost boundary, and the consumer sweep for a new always-on stream explicitly includes collaborating parties, peers and upstreams.

Previously: v2.6.72 (2026-08-02) — **A peer's own vocabulary made the row preview blank, and a blank preview looks exactly like a message with nothing to say (Constellation v2.4.137)** — each A2A intent carried a hand-listed set of summary keys, so a counterpart writing its own dialect — measured: `TOPIC` where this side writes `re` — matched nothing and the row rendered as a bare label. That absence is the expensive part: an empty preview and a contentless message are the same shape on screen, so the list being stale cannot be seen from the surface it damages. Rather than growing twenty per-intent lists, the resolution is structural in three layers. **One shared tail** follows each intent's own ordering, so a newly observed dialect is one edit in one place rather than twenty; `topic` sits beside `re` because it fills the same role. **Case is folded during lookup**, since capitalisation is itself a dialect axis and keeping two spellings of every key is the same enumeration problem wearing a different hat. And a **last resort** picks the first field that reads like prose — never leaving the row blank — while recording which key it had to guess from, so the vocabulary gap becomes a collected observation instead of a silent one (`window.wsDialectKeys`). The prose preference is not decoration: the first field is arbitrary, and a live counterpart's first field was a content hash, so a preview showed sixty-four hex characters where a sentence was available two fields later. Preference runs sentence → prose-like → anything, which on the same live payload moved the preview from a model identifier to the actual finding. Verified against both live dialects and the regression case where the canonical field still wins when present.

Previously: v2.6.71 (2026-08-02) — **An address answers «where from», and the operator surface needs «who» (Constellation v2.4.136, §13.25.17)** — the operator predicate reads a connection's address: from this machine means holds the board. Behind the reverse proxy the specification itself recommends, every remote client presents as local, so the previous cut could only *refuse* that case — detection, not a path. This cut supplies the path as a reference implementation, and its whole design is the additive contract: **with zero accounts the layer does not exist** — predicate, cookie and screen bit-identical to before, no file created, a login attempt answered «no operators» rather than a prompt. Adoption is decided by one act with no flag: creating the first account. An adopter who never creates one is not carrying a disabled feature, they are carrying the previous behaviour. With at least one account the predicate consults the session **and nothing else**, because a predicate that keeps an address fallback is one the proxy can still flip. The requirements each match a way this class fails: memory-hard salted hashing, constant-time comparison, equal cost for a missing account (or response time enumerates accounts), per-account attempt limiting, an in-memory session token so a restart logs everyone out rather than adding a second secret at rest, `HttpOnly`+`SameSite=Strict` cookies with `Secure` off loopback, and the account file in the credential class — gitignored and `0600`. Bootstrap has one exception that closes itself: account creation is unauthenticated only while the count is zero, so the window is one act wide. Deleting the last account is refused through the API for the same reason it remains possible on disk — it silently returns the deployment to address-based judgement, which is a decision rather than a click. Display names are attacker-controlled free text and are escaped at render, since rendering them as markup is stored XSS against *other* operators — the case a single-operator deployment structurally cannot notice. The checker runs two isolated servers and asserts both arms: zero-account behaviour unchanged, and the verdict **flipping** the moment an account exists, which is what makes «the layer is wired» measurable rather than assumed; a mutation run confirmed it goes red when the predicate line is removed. Found while verifying: a frame addressed to its own sender was being registered for redelivery, and since a receiving bridge deliberately never acks its own echo, that entry could never clear — a guaranteed false «unreachable» three attempts later. The main bridge's own connect greeting was taking that path on every reconnect, so an alert meaning «could not deliver» actually meant «addressed to myself».

Previously: v2.6.70 (2026-08-02) — **A pair-shaped split for a two-tab board, still hardcoded at four tabs — and markdown that stopped at the features its first reader needed (Constellation v2.4.134–135)** — Ctrl+clicking a tab was supposed to toggle *that* tab into the split view. It always produced the same fixed pair instead, and nobody could see it while there were only two tabs, because that pair was the only combination the intent could produce. At four tabs the difference became visible: the code said «pair», never «toggle». Splitting is now genuinely N-way — any combination, up to four panes, dividers generated between neighbours, the last pane protected from removal, and layout order following tab order rather than click order, because a set that lands in a different arrangement each time never becomes muscle memory. Sizing follows the pane count: two panes keep the existing ratio-and-pin contract untouched, three or more use per-pane weights where dragging a divider moves weight between its two neighbours only. Home affordances now derive their position from **their own pane's right edge** rather than from «the divider», which was well-defined only while there was exactly one. Compendium term links open the wiki **beside** what you were reading instead of replacing it — the previous behaviour cost you the screen you were consulting the term for — and narrow viewports still swap rather than cram. Second, markdown: the renderer was a chat-only miniature, so tables, ordered lists, quotes, rules and diagrams simply were not there — and «not there» surfaced only as pipe characters sitting in the text, which reads as a styling problem rather than a missing feature. It now renders the full set, mermaid included, across the review panel and the live conversation alike, keeping the escape-first discipline (escape the source, then build tags; diagram source is injected as text, never as markup). One ordering bug is worth recording because it is invisible in the common case: restoring code and diagram placeholders **after** the newline-to-break conversion put break tags inside the diagram source, so mermaid failed to parse and code blocks came out with markup in them — the fix is that newlines convert first and placeholders restore last. Verified in a live browser at every step: four panes with three dividers, arbitrary combinations, table/ordered-list/quote/rule/code and a rendered diagram.

Previously: v2.6.69 (2026-08-02) — **Typing cost 185ms per keystroke, and the board was paying it in full layout recalculations (Constellation v2.4.133)** — the operator reported input in the review panel lagging tens of seconds before landing in one burst. Three synthetic passes failed to reproduce it — idle, under CPU load, at several window widths — and the failure was informative: automated typing skips IME composition entirely, so it never paid the per-jamo cost that makes the symptom visible. The operator's own observation closed it: *narrow the window and it appears*. Measured with a scripted input sweep, the cost was **165–195 ms per keystroke** at every width, worst when narrow — a full second of stall per five characters, and Korean composition emits an event per jamo, which is exactly «tens of seconds, then all at once». The mechanism has two halves that are easy to blame on each other. The textarea's auto-grow sets height to `auto`, reads `scrollHeight`, then writes the measured height — a write-read-write that forces **two full document layouts, synchronously, on every input event**. And one full layout on this page costs ~30 ms, because the visible dashboard pane renders every completed entry at once: 7,260 nodes of 8,356. Neither half is a defect alone; together they multiply. The fix addresses both, and an A/B/A distinguished the effective ingredient from the plausible one: `content-visibility: auto` on cards took it from 184.4 to 0.7 ms/key and reverting restored 177.6, while `contain: layout paint style` alone measured 196.7 — no help at all. Skipping layout for off-screen cards is the active ingredient; containment without it is a plausible remedy that does nothing. The auto-grow is separately coalesced to one measurement per animation frame, because typing cannot outrun a frame, so the visible result is identical and the count drops. The two are not substitutes: one cuts how often layout is forced, the other cuts what each one costs. Verified after deployment at both narrow and wide widths: **0 ms per keystroke**, 310 cards still rendering with none collapsed.

Previously: v2.6.68 (2026-08-02) — **Four implementations refused the same ack for the same reason — that's a specification gap wearing four disguises (Constellation v2.4.132)** — the relay shipped hours earlier delivered, and then delivered again, and again: every operator-feedback frame arrived in triplicate, ~35 seconds apart, same event id, same recovery key. The redelivery layer was doing its job — the commitment ack never came back. §13.13.2 said the receiver replies «to the original sender», and that wording named only the case its author had in mind: a frame relayed by the server on a board surface's behalf carries no sender `agentId`, and **all four bridge implementations** — the canonical bridge, both main-bridge channels, and the light join helpers — declined to ack it on the same guard, «no addressee for the ack». Four independent implementations stumbling at one spot is the deterministic signature of a spec gap, not four coincident bugs. The rule now stated for all cases: the ack predicate keys on (addressed to me + recovery key + not an ack kind) and MUST NOT condition on a resolvable sender; with no addressee the ack simply omits `targetAgentId`, and the server consumes a target-less `AckProcessed` after clearing the pending entry — the absorb matters, because the unaddressed fallback would otherwise deliver transport plumbing to the main agent as mail. Two receiver corollaries came out of nearly shipping a second defect inside the first fix: a redelivered duplicate is **re-acked** — the redelivery itself proves the previous ack was not seen, so a dedupe that swallows the duplicate silently *extends* the loop it was meant to end — and its body is not re-appended (the canonical bridge already carried the LRU for this; the light helpers gained a bounded set). A checker axis now asserts the optional-target idiom on all five deployed copies plus the server absorb. Wire-measured before and after: the same POST that landed three times now lands exactly once, with the ack recorded server-consumed.

Previously: v2.6.67 (2026-08-02) — **The operator answered, and nobody was listening: the file is the log, the relay is the wake (Constellation v2.4.131)** — the operator pushed five substantive entries through the dashboard's feedback buttons — answers to three open decisions and two defect reports — and the agent heard none of them, because `POST /api/feedback` appended to `feedback.jsonl` and appending was the whole pipeline: no relay, no watcher, no consumer. The header comment even said «server log → agent review» — a designed intent whose enforcing mechanism didn't exist, which is what a rule without a mechanism looks like. The bitter detail: one of the five entries *reported the wake failure*, and fell into the very hole it described. §13.13.4 now makes the relay normative: the server relays every accepted entry into the live stream as an `OperatorFeedback` event targeted at the primary agent — carrying a recovery key from the shared stamping component so a main-bridge outage redelivers instead of dropping, timestamp-normalized at the broadcast boundary, mirrored to boards, persisted to history, offered to web-push.
