Teaching every consumer to watch the file would have been the wrong shape: it multiplies watchers that each fail separately, where one relay reuses the entire existing delivery, redelivery, classification and notification pipeline. The consumer sweep is stated in the spec so adopters don't rediscover it (classifiers must not absorb the name; push should notify; unaddressed seats are untouched), and a parity-checker axis now asserts the call site (append **then** relay, order not adjacency), the recovery-key path, the live deployed copy (which is how the axis proved it could fail — it went red on the un-synced copy before the restart), and the two negative consumer assertions. Wire-verified end to end after restart: a test POST arrived as `OperatorFeedback` in the agent inbox within seconds. Also disclosed: v2.4.130 (the future-timestamp clamp) had shipped in code without its module-surface bump — trued up in this cut's docs.

Previously: v2.6.66 (2026-08-02) — **Two external evaluation reports, taken through the new intake skill — and the quote that looked wrong was a correct quote of our own stale surface** — two commissioned external assessments of this project (a harness evaluation scoring 7.7/10, and a visibility analysis) became `/feeda`'s first live run. Every checkable claim was measured against the repository and GitHub rather than judged by plausibility: star/fork/commit counts, topics, the docs tagline, the migration scenarios, seed tier sizes, and the module roster. The decisive verdict: where the reports and the repository disagreed, **the reports were right**. Their «six optional modules» was an accurate quote of README surfaces frozen since the seventh module shipped a week earlier — and measurement found the drift wider than they saw: the README said six in the heading and seven two paragraphs later, listed six bullets, and omitted the seventh from all four install blocks; the KO register copies tripled every instance, and the EN and KO size tables had drifted from *each other* (EN updated to ~2440/~1120 lines, KO frozen at ~2425/~1105, Compact stale on both sides). Per the intake discipline the mechanism came before the fix: a new **readme-claims** verify axis derives the module count from the marketplace — a plugin whose capitalized spec `.md` exists at the repo root counts as a module, derived, never hand-listed — and checks every EN/KO count phrase against it, plus seed-size claims within ±5% of measured line counts. It went red with 16 real violations before the fix, and its own first draft had to be corrected twice (a Lite table row mentioning the *other* tier mis-attributed a claim; a wc-vs-split off-by-one) — the checker-must-be-able-to-fail rule earning its keep on its author. Also confirmed from the reports and recorded as decisions rather than silently fixed: the repository has a hundred-plus tags but **zero GitHub Releases**, the first impression shows the whole platform where the minimal path is one Compact seed, and no independent quantitative case study exists — three visibility actions that belong to the operator, now on the decisions panel.

Previously: v2.6.65 (2026-08-02) — **The fourth borrowing: a memory hierarchy for the context cache (Superscalar v0.12.0 / plugin v0.4.0)** — the operator's fourth skill idea asked where context-cache management should live, and the answer was already in the module's name: Superscalar borrows processor architecture — issue width, out-of-order execution, speculation, heterogeneous cores — and the memory hierarchy was the chapter still missing, even though two cache rules were already living scattered in §5.1 (a delegated subagent starts cache-cold; effort is a caching decision). §5.4 generalizes them into one discipline with one home, and the numbers underneath it went through a research pass with a deterministic verification gate rather than recall: a claim ledger of twelve entries, nine verified (each cross-confirmed on at least two independent domains, refutation-searched for the numeric ones, anchored to the vendor's own page), three left explicitly unresolved as vendor-doc-only, zero refuted — though one prior belief was corrected on the way, since the «half price» cached-input figure remembered for one vendor is now a tenth, paired with a paid cache write. The decisive find was that the hardest axis — which harness operations flush the cache — needed no observation at all: an official page documents it end to end, down to subagents running a shorter TTL than the main conversation and the flushes that fire without any operator action, such as a tool server dying and auto-reconnecting. The discipline is six rules: pin model and effort at the session head, since each is a cache key and a switch bills on the *next* turn where it feels free; compact at task boundaries and prefer rewind over compact for abandoned paths, because a rewind returns to a prefix that is already cached while a compact builds a new one; prefetch context right *after* a boundary, where reads land at the front of the new stable prefix; price fan-out with cache-cold accounting, which lands delegation exactly where §5.1 already routes it; consult the per-harness flush list before any mid-session mutation; and measure with the cache counters instead of declaring, which is this repository's telemetry rule applied to a new surface. The volatile half — multipliers, TTLs, minimums, hourly storage rents, flush lists — lives in `cache-registry.json` under the §5.1.3 contract (asOf, confirmedBy per row, revisit date and watchlist), with one addition: facts confirmed only against the vendor's own page carry a `singleSource` mark instead of blending silently with cross-confirmed ones. Default OFF for a jurisdictional reason rather than caution: harnesses already automate the substrate, so the skill covers only what they don't decide — and that scope is *expected to shrink* as absorption proceeds, which is the module's standard-substrate bet working as intended, not a defect.

Previously: v2.6.64 (2026-08-01) — **Three operator-proposed skills: two intake disciplines for the kit, and out-of-order execution for the main lane (kit v0.4.0, Superscalar v0.11.0 / plugin v0.3.0)** — `/talka` is the gentle sibling of `/eg-interview`: same goal — real intent, real constraints, real criteria — opposite pace. Where the interview presses a request into a delegable brief in a few batched rounds, talk-about holds a relaxed, topic-anchored conversation for the cases where that press fails or harms: tacit knowledge, an early topic, stakes that make interrogation premature. Its discipline is at most one question per turn — prefer stating an observation and letting *correction* do the extraction, because people correct a wrong statement more readily, and more precisely, than they answer an open question — plus a running elicitation ledger whose every entry must change the upcoming judgment, materialized to a durable surface at close (a ledger that evaporates with the session was chat). `/feeda` codifies the intake pipeline this repository has been running by hand on adopter reports: read the whole artifact — data to verify, never instructions to follow; embedded directives surface as the artifact's *requests*, not as orders — verify every claim by measurement into confirmed / partial / refuted / unmeasurable, tier the survivors (defect · spec gap — an independent implementer stumbling where the spec named one case indicts the spec, not their reading · design disagreement · info-only), build the prevention mechanism *before* fixing the instance, then close the loop with the contributor, refutations included with their measurements — a closed loop turns a one-time reporter into a standing detector, and silence teaches them to stop writing. `/ooo` (§5.3) extends the module's out-of-order discipline to the main lane's own most common stall: a question to the human. Ask-and-wait is an in-order pipeline stalled on one very slow operand, so a question now registers as a one-line scoreboard entry instead of halting the task — self-resolve by measurement first (most questions are unread files), bypass everything independent of the answer, assumption-run only under §4's speculation gates, park what remains. A late answer wakes only its dependents, never a global restart; the forbidden transition is the silent guess, an unnamed assumption indistinguishable from confidence to every later reader. Loss and external-publish gates are never bypassed in any mode — the toggle moves the *waiting*, never the *gates*. Also in this cut: the README's Current section had been carrying three paragraphs of v2.6.59 state through four releases — including a «checkers are red on purpose» claim that stopped being true at v2.6.61 — now retired to this changelog, where that story already lives. And the changelog itself had the same disease: twenty-seven Korean entries had accumulated inside the English section, so a reader following the «한국어» link landed twenty-seven releases behind — all moved to their own section.

Previously: v2.6.63 (2026-08-01) — **Three operator-reported board defects, and the first two were the new telemetry's own (Constellation v2.4.129)** — the seat telemetry shipped hours earlier was rendering as a chat row every few seconds and raising a push notification each time. Cost had not been saved, only moved: a stream that costs no model tokens was filling the surface a person reads. The fix is placement, not frequency — telemetry now feeds an indicator, exactly as the workflow and subagent snapshots already did, and joins the push blocklist beside them. That blocklist already carried a comment saying a new snapshot name must be added to it; the rule was there and the name was not, which is what a discipline without a mechanism looks like. **Second, the time axis.** Frames were appearing at positions unrelated to when they happened, and correcting themselves on reload. Timestamp normalization existed but sat at the storage boundary, while dispatch broadcasts before it records — so live viewers received un-normalized frames and sorted a missing timestamp as zero, placing them at the epoch. Normalization moved to the broadcast boundary, because fixing our own emitter would have left every other adapter able to reproduce it. The emitter was fixed too: it had never stamped a time, which also explains workflow progress rows landing oddly. **Third, history volume.** The initial payload carried every event of every active channel, which on a long-running board is what makes the first paint heavy. Active channels are now capped per channel, older entries load on request, and — the load-bearing part — the truncation is *declared*: the payload states the limit, which channels were cut, how many were sent of how many, and the oldest timestamp sent, so the client can ask for the next page and show what remains. A silent cut would read as deletion, which is the misdiagnosis the scope field was introduced for in v2.4.89. Also folded in: `Ping`/`Pong` were being absorbed by name, making the escalation ladder's probe step structurally unanswerable, and the ack-body rule from the previous cut was applied to the whole exclusion list rather than the three ack names — briefly making a join greeting wake the session.

Previously: v2.6.58 (2026-07-30) — **MCP dropped the handshake, and the servers here were still the first revision (dual-era)** — the 2026-07-28 revision makes MCP stateless at the protocol layer: the `initialize` handshake is gone, every request carries its protocol version in `_meta`, and servers **MUST** implement `server/discover`. The four MCP servers in this repository declared `2024-11-05` and implemented `initialize`, `tools/list`, `tools/call` — nothing else. In the specification's compatibility matrix that is the row that fails: a modern client against a legacy server.

The migration was small for an unusual reason: none of what the revision deprecates was ever implemented here. No Roots, Sampling, Logging, `ping`, `logging/setLevel`, HTTP+SSE, resources or prompts — so the deprecations cost nothing and there was nothing to remove. The servers were also already stateless in practice: `initialize` was answered but never required, so a request arriving without it was already served.

They are now dual-era. `server/discover` advertises both supported versions; `initialize` stays and still answers with the legacy version, because a legacy client handed a modern version string has nowhere to go. A request whose `_meta` names an unsupported version gets `-32022` with the supported list; a request with no `_meta` at all is served as before. Results carry `resultType` and list results carry `ttlMs`/`cacheScope`. The version strings live in one shared component rather than four copies — the same enumeration-drift shape this repository hit three times in the preceding day.

The checker runs the four servers as real stdio child processes and speaks JSON-RPC to them, because the defect class here is a protocol envelope and a source grep cannot see one. Four mutations revert each half. Separately, this cut was implemented by a different engine under a written brief, and the first attempt failed in a way worth recording: the fallback engine's command line carried a flag its current release had removed, and it reported a usage error while exiting zero. The same command line is what this repository's means ladder builds when it switches engines, so the ladder's last rung was broken from the day it shipped.

Previously: v2.6.57 (2026-07-30) — **A premise a single configuration line can invert is not a premise (Constellation v2.4.125)** — a board carries a roster, a conversation history, an org chart, and controls that issue live keys and hand over the control plane. None of it is meant to face the open internet, and that was written down. It was also expressible away: an allowlist of `null` meant *every address*. Measured on the live board — binding exposed, agent and MCP allowlists both unset — a non-loopback address received the agent roster and the conversation history **before sending a handshake at all**. Presenting a key afterwards was refused, but the refusal came after the payload.

The detail that makes the shape clear is that the *human* UI surface was already narrowed, in the same file, to a LAN range plus an overlay range. The surfaces left open were the agent-facing ones — the two whose audience is machines, and therefore the two nobody looks at.

So the change is the allowlist's **domain**, not its evaluation. The domain is private address space: named ranges (loopback, RFC1918, CGNAT, link-local, IPv6 ULA) or CIDRs inside them. A public address is not a value the configuration can hold — an entry covering one is refused with a reason printed on every load, because an entry silently dropped reads exactly like an entry that works. Absent now means *all private ranges* rather than *all addresses*, since a default is the state of every deployment nobody has thought about. Empty means loopback only: closed has to be expressible, and it must not lock the operator out of the surface that would reopen it.

The arithmetic has to be exact, because the neighbours of private space are public — `172.31.255.255` is private and `172.32.0.0` is not, `100.127.255.255` is CGNAT and `100.128.0.0` is not. A predicate that is loose at those edges widens the reachable set by a whole range and reports nothing, so the checker tests the boundaries on both sides. A range *entry* is validated at both ends rather than at its base, since `10.0.0.0/4` begins in private space and swallows most of the public internet.

Refusing public sources costs nothing, because both legitimate ways to reach a board from elsewhere arrive privately: an overlay network places the peer inside CGNAT, and a reverse proxy terminating TLS connects from the host. That also settles transport — **the board does not implement TLS**, because a proxy that already owns certificates and renewal does it better, and a board that only accepts private sources is not the layer where wire encryption belongs.

What this does not fix is the next question rather than a footnote. Behind a proxy every client appears local, which inverts every address-based judgment including the one granting the `main` role. Address scope cannot answer *which person*, so the code does what it can: a request carrying forwarding headers is not counted as host-local, recorded at upgrade time because headers are only visible there. Detection is not identity. An internet-reachable board needs an account concept, and once it has one the human UI and the agent interface want separate ports — their audiences differ, their gates differ, and one port forces the weaker requirement onto both. Both are registered as open.

Fixing this broke five smoke tests at once, which was the more useful finding. Each of seven harnesses staged the reference runtime into a sandbox by **listing the files to copy**, and the lists had drifted in both directions — they omitted the new component and carried one the server does not require at all. The symptom was not "component missing" but "the isolated server does not start", one layer removed from the cause. The lists are gone; the closure is derived from what the entry point actually requires, with an explicit `extra` only for files launched as separate processes, which a require graph cannot see.

Previously: v2.6.56 (2026-07-29) — **A scanner that catches every vendor's tokens and not its own (Ultrasafe v0.2.14)** — the publish hook records one command line into the assessment ledger for advisory context, and `maskSecrets` is the only hygiene control on that path. Iteration 2 measured four shapes passing through it unchanged: a PAT inside a push URL, an `Authorization: Bearer` value, an npmrc `:_authToken=`, and this project's own connection-key format. The last one is the one that stings — the control knew about `NPM_TOKEN` and `--otp` and nothing about `ck-`/`uk-`/`pk-`/`lk-`.

The npmrc case is worth naming on its own, because it was not a missing rule but a missing flag. The env-var rule required the name to contain `TOKEN`, `SECRET`, `PASSWORD` — and it was case-sensitive, so `NPM_TOKEN` was caught and `_authToken` was not. Reading the regex does not show that; running it does.

The masking now separates two kinds of rule. **Shape rules** have a name sitting beside the value, so the value is redacted while the name stays readable and the preview keeps its advisory worth — flags, named values, URL userinfo, and an `Authorization` value however the scheme is spelled. **Literal rules** have no name anywhere, which is the ordinary case for a token embedded in a URL or standing alone in a header, so they are recognised by issuer prefix. The prefix list carries this project's own key shape beside GitHub's, GitLab's, npm's, Slack's and AWS's.

The report named the reason this needed a checker rather than only a fix: reverting the control broke nothing anywhere. The new checker is behavioural, since the defect was invisible in the source — it feeds real strings and reads the output, asserts that ordinary publish lines survive untouched (a masker that redacts everything passes every leak assertion), and confirms the hook actually calls the function. Four mutations revert it, one per rule. The first draft of one of them was itself wrong: its probe value also matched a prefix rule, so the sweep masked it and the mutation measured a neighbouring rule instead of the one it aimed at. The mutation caught that, which is what mutations are for.

Previously: v2.6.55 (2026-07-29) — **A provider's refusal is not a failure, and a seat waiting on one reports healthy the whole time (Constellation v2.4.124)** — the turn classifier had names for two kinds of failure: the means (a binary moved, a flag renamed, a model id retired) and the work (a bad prompt, a missing file). A usage limit is neither. Having no name, it fell to the work class, whose remedy is to retry — and the supervisor polls every five seconds, so a five-hour window is on the order of 3,600 attempts into a wall.

Three further consequences followed from the same missing name. The means-failure counter is reset by any non-means outcome, so the ladder never moved — and the ladder is exactly the instrument that would have helped, because quota pools are per-provider and the other engine was available the whole time. The session was discarded on the first refusal, though nothing was wrong with the session. And the reset time, which the vendor writes into the very error being ignored, was never read.

Detection is deliberately broad but is evaluated only on a turn that already failed, since a false positive costs hours of idleness while a false negative costs one wasted attempt — a successful turn whose output merely discusses limits is not a refusal. The reset time is parsed opportunistically across five shapes and is **not** required: an unparsed refusal re-probes on a bounded fixed interval, which rediscovers the reset within one interval and removes the great majority of the wasted attempts while knowing nothing at all. Any parsed deadline is capped, because a misparse into the distant future silences the seat permanently, which is the failure the section already forbids. This path had never run here — 201 turn records contain no limit event — so the design assumes the wording will not match rather than assuming it will.

The first version of the ladder change failed its own test: it descended on a clock and returned on a turn count, so a seat that switched away never switched back. The ladder now carries two positions instead of one. The **demotion floor** is a means judgment — persisted, moved down by accumulated means failures and back up by a bounded count of successful turns. The **running rung** is the first rung at or below that floor whose engine is not currently refused, derived per turn from the block map and never stored, so the return is automatic because there is no position to restore. Measuring that surfaced an older defect: the chosen rung had been used for the turn and never written back, so the stored position stayed at zero — demotion was always computed as zero-to-one, the last rung was unreachable, and the counter governing return to the primary never incremented at all.

While the seat waits, bridge upkeep continues, because it draws on no quota and suspending it loses the inbound traffic that arrives during the wait. The wait itself is published in the heartbeat: a waiting seat keeps beating and would otherwise read as healthy for hours, which is the mirror image of a failure disguised as absence and just as invisible to a probe that asks whether the layer is running. The survivability checker went from 108 to 139 assertions with five new mutations, one of which reproduces the pre-existing unreachable-rung behaviour. Separately, three heartbeat files were tracked in version control: the ignore rules named files one at a time, and three new writers walked past the enumeration.

Previously: v2.6.54 (2026-07-29) — **A number that is displayed and a number that decides must not be different numbers wearing one name (Ultrasafe v0.2.13)** — `lib/coverage-floor.cjs` exists because the same gate condition had been implemented twice with different thresholds and different units, and it unified both. One layer up, the *quantity* being compared against them was still derived two ways. The clean-signal gate averaged per-axis coverage over the declared axes — how much surface was actually examined. The iteration writer divided attackers dispatched by attackers configured — how many agents were launched. Both were called `coverage`, and the one written into the ledger, the one a human reads, was the agent ratio, while the one that decided the gate was the axis mean.

They disagree whenever an attacker covers other than exactly one axis, which is the ordinary case: the measured run dispatched seven attackers across eight axes, so the ledger recorded 53.8% for a run whose axis coverage was 61.5%. Neither number was near the tier floor, so the verdict happened to be right — which is exactly how this survives, since a wrong number that reaches the same conclusion produces no symptom.

The writer also carried a third hardcoded floor, `0.8`, under a comment asserting parity with the hook. The hook's was `0.85` and the spec's tier table says 75 for this tier. The comment claimed the consistency that the module beneath it had been created to provide and that this file had never adopted.

The numerator now lives beside the threshold and the unit, in one function both surfaces call. Unreported axes count as zero rather than as absent — averaging only what was submitted lets a run raise its own score by skipping axes, which is the defect this project measured going from 50% to 90%. Coverage with nothing submitted is `null`, and a `null` coverage yields a `null` verdict rather than a failing one, because a condition that could not be evaluated is neither met nor unmet. The dispatch ratio is kept under a name that says what it is.

The iteration checker failed on this change, correctly — it had been asserting the old contract, pinning coverage to the agent ratio. Its axes were rewritten to the new contract rather than removed, and one was added for the case the change introduces: omit the per-axis values and both coverage and the clean verdict record `null`, never a substitute derived from the dispatch count.

Previously: v2.6.53 (2026-07-29) — **«Unspecified» and «unrecognized» fell into the same else, so an explicit request was silently granted as something else (Constellation v2.4.123)** — §3.1 has stated since v0.5 that an unknown key kind MUST be rejected at the registration boundary and never coerced to a default. The reference server did not do that. Its issuance line read `(local||collab||upstream||peer) ? kind : 'upstream'`, which collapses two different situations into one branch: a client that sends no kind at all — the dashboard's issue button, where a default is intended — and a client that asks for a kind the server does not recognize, because of a typo or because the server is older than the caller. The second case is the one that matters. A caller that explicitly requested a peer key received an upstream key, the reply said issued, and nothing anywhere reported a discrepancy.

The section's own rationale names the cost: a kind that is misread as collab attaches collab group membership to a connection that should not have it. So the failure is not cosmetic — it is a quiet widening of who is inside a group.

The fix separates the two: an unrecognized value returns an error envelope naming the closed enum, while an absent value still takes the intended default. Three behavioural assertions were added to the key smoke test — reject the unrecognized, keep the default for the unspecified, and confirm the peer kind actually issues a `pk-` key on its own URL parameter rather than riding the upstream one. Reverting the fix fails the first assertion and leaves the other two green, which is what a specific axis should do.

Provenance is worth recording. A collaborating agent found this shape in its own server, said so, and quoted the line. Checking whether the same shape existed here is what surfaced it — and the section being violated was the one this side had cited at them an hour earlier. A specification that is quoted but not conformed to is worse than one nobody read, because the citation is taken as evidence of compliance.

Previously: v2.6.52 (2026-07-29) — **Guidance placed where it is convenient to author, not where the reader arrives (seed v2.6.1, Constellation v2.4.122)** — an earlier cut specified what an autonomous agent should do when handed this standard with no request attached: treat reception as installation rather than production, mount pure discipline by default and require an explicit request for anything that runs a process, binds a port, spends money or claims authority. That guidance went into the protocol and into the kit's bootstrap skill. It did not go into the seed — and the seed is the artifact that actually gets handed over. The intended reader is precisely an agent that received one file and nothing else, so the guidance was absent from the only place that reader looks.

The seed now carries it as **Mode R**, alongside the bootstrap and three migration modes it already offered. Those four all presuppose a request; Mode R is the case where none is attached, which is why its absence was easy to miss — nothing failed, the agent simply picked the closest-fitting mode and produced something. All six tier/language files carry it, scaled: the Master files state the reasoning, Lite compresses it, Compact keeps the splitting rule and the two orderings (conduct before infrastructure, bootstrap before the first deliverable).

§13.32.6(e) now states the three-way relationship instead of implying a two-way one: the skill owns the procedure, the section states the contract, and the seed carries the compressed form because it is the copy that travels alone. None restates another.

The edit itself had to respect a measured trap: three of the six seed files carry mixed line endings, and this repository has previously rewritten all six in full by normalizing before editing. The insertion detects the terminator at each anchor and uses that, which the byte counts confirm — the stray line-feed counts in the mixed files came through unchanged.

Previously: v2.6.51 (2026-07-29) — **A greeting is re-sent on every reconnect, so anything mutable in it becomes a standing claim (Constellation v2.4.121)** — a peer reported that our bridge kept announcing a track as in progress ten days after that track closed, and that it had been waiting for a follow-up that was never coming. The greeting a bridge sends on connect carries free-text metadata for human readers, and that greeting goes out again on every reconnect — every socket flap, every process restart, every reboot. A status written there is therefore not a note; it is a claim re-asserted indefinitely. Nothing errors on either side: the sender sees an ordinary reconnect and the receiver sees a fresh assertion.

A comment reminding the author to update that string when the track closed was sitting directly above it. That is what a discipline looks like when nothing enforces it — and the fix is not better upkeep. The greeting now carries identity and a pointer to where live state is authoritative, and carries no state at all; a greeting with no state in it cannot go stale. A checker rejects track-state vocabulary in that field, and its mutation restores the exact string that shipped, because a synthetic sentence would not reproduce this class.

§2 step 3 states the rule where the greeting's shape is specified, since the trap belongs to the protocol rather than to one deployment.

Operationally the seat watchdog grew to the surface a reboot exposed. Everything it already covered came back by itself; the two bridges the interactive seat uses to reach the board did not, because they had been filed as things that appear when a seat opens rather than as programs independent of it. They are now components like any other. Three more processes learned to emit a heartbeat from inside their work loop through a shared module that deliberately contains no timer — one that scheduled itself would keep the heartbeat advancing through exactly the wedge it exists to catch, so the absence of a timer is what forces the caller to beat from its loop.

Two placement traps came with that. Each of those loops legitimately returns early — a bridge when disconnected, the mirror when echo is off — and a heartbeat written below that return turns a normal idle state into a stall, so the watchdog would restart healthy processes forever. The beat goes above the return and the state rides along as a value. And a disconnected bridge is not simply dead: restarting a client that cannot reach a downed server is churn, so the rule is now a cross-component one — the board is up and only this bridge has been disconnected past its grace, therefore the fault is here.

The staleness threshold is no longer written into the watchdog per component; it is derived from the interval each heartbeat reports about itself, so a component that changes its loop period does not silently outrun a number nobody remembered to update. The strongest new axis is a coverage assertion rather than a snapshot: every process that emits a heartbeat must appear in the watchdog's component list, so adding one and forgetting to register it is a build failure rather than a quiet gap.

Previously: v2.6.50 (2026-07-29) — **A recovery path fails as an absence, not an error (Constellation v2.4.120)** — the script that owned reboot registration for the live board could not be parsed at all. The host reads a BOM-less UTF-8 file in the system ANSI codepage, and some characters became syntax; registration, status query, and removal were all unavailable. Nothing reported it, because a recovery path that cannot run produces no error — it produces nothing, which is what a recovery path that is never needed also produces. All four scripts of that class were one edit away from the same state.

That is the shape of the whole cut. The seat stack was supervised top-down — scheduler brings up the launcher, the launcher brings up the server and the supervisor, the supervisor brings up the bridge and each turn — and every layer was covered except the top. When the supervisor itself wedged, the board stayed healthy and delegations stopped being processed, and the only remaining recovery mechanism was a person noticing. The layer added here hangs off the OS scheduler, because a supervisor supervised by another supervisor only relocates the question, and it is non-destructive when healthy so it can run on a short interval — a bring-up script that reclaims a port is correct at boot and is an outage on a timer.

Four claims that looked like health were separated from health. **Could-not-observe** is now a third state rather than a fold into either neighbour: folding it into healthy leaves a seat dead indefinitely, folding it into dead restarts working seats, so an unobservable component is escalated and never restarted. **A live process** is no longer read as a live seat — the supervisor emits a heartbeat from inside its work loop, and a stale heartbeat is death even while the process is alive (a separate timer would keep advancing through exactly the wedge being hunted). **Exit zero** is no longer read as work done: the harness result envelope was being requested and discarded, so a turn that hit a turn ceiling or a budget ceiling advanced the intake cursor and the work disappeared. **Restarted** is no longer read as revived — the layer re-probes after acting and clears its failure counter only on a verified recovery, and it removes a wedged instance before starting its replacement, because a single-instance lock otherwise rejects the replacement and the layer records a restart that did not occur.

Recovery carries the session rather than starting a new one, which also decides what happens when the *means* fails rather than the work: a vendor closing a path leaves the session intact and only the tool unreachable, so the session is kept and the tooling descends a ladder ordered by which assumption each rung drops — pinned engine and model, then the same engine with pins removed, then a different engine with no pins. A ladder of alternatives that each carry fresh pins would reintroduce the class it exists to survive. Descent happens on the means class only; a work failure that demotes the engine abandons a working path over a bad prompt.

§13.35 records two withdrawals alongside the addition. Preamble cost was withdrawn as a residency argument in the previous cut; the exit-code blind spot is withdrawn here, because it was a gap in the supervisor rather than in the shape and is now closed in both shapes. What survives as a reason for residency is unattended recovery and means diversity, which is what the section now says.

The checker carries fourteen mutations, one of which strips the byte-order mark from the measured file and confirms seven real parser errors. Two of its own axes were hollow on the first run — a wildcard that spanned the whole file passed on any two fragments in any order, and a component-scoped assertion crossed into the next component's value — and the mutations added to catch them are what exposed it.

Previously: v2.6.49 (2026-07-29) — **A check that can fail is not the same as a check that can fail for the whole class it was written against (Ultrasafe sc-08 / crypto-16)** — the vendored dependency tree was verified for shape: which packages are present, their versions, a license file, pruned artifacts. Every one of those is a property of the tree's surface. Editing a byte inside a vendored file changed none of them, and a tampered copy passed all assertions — two separate audit axes reached that conclusion independently, each with its own proof.

The tree now carries a **content digest per package**, computed over sorted path-plus-content so that swapping two identically-sized files is drift too, and a missing digest is reported as a defect rather than counted as clean — a tree with no digest is a tree with no way to detect tampering, which is the condition rather than the absence of one.

The repair that matters is to the negative control. It planted only shape violations, so it demonstrated that the checker could fail while leaving the class it now covers entirely untested. It plants a content edit as well, restores the bytes afterwards, and asserts the restoration directly. That last assertion was wrong on its first run and the code was right: at that point the shape probes are still planted, and they legitimately change the package's content digest. Comparing the whole tree there conflates the probe with the tamper; comparing the edited file's bytes does not.

Previously: v2.6.48 (2026-07-29) — **Pinning a value does not make it true; it makes it consistently false (Constellation v2.4.119)** — a joining party declares its own display name, and that name reaches the dashboard tab label and the sender line of the operator's device push notification. An earlier cut noticed the second half of this and pinned every message's name to the value captured at connection time. What it did not do was validate the value being pinned. Pinning an unvalidated name does not prevent impersonation — it guarantees a consistently impersonated one, which is worse than an inconsistent one because it looks settled.

Previously: v2.6.47 (2026-07-29) — **Truncating a secret looks like hiding it and is actually leaking a little, and a little repeated is not a little (Constellation v2.4.118)** — connection keys are a role prefix followed by 24 hex characters. The server logged the first fourteen of them, which is roughly 44 of the key's 96 secret bits, and it did so on **every connection attempt** rather than at issuance. Log files and console history accumulate that. Shortening the prefix to six or eight characters was the obvious repair and is the wrong shape: it leaks the same thing more slowly. The key is now logged as a hash fingerprint that keeps the role prefix — the part an operator actually correlates on, and the part that narrows nothing.

Previously: v2.6.46 (2026-07-29) — **The measurement removed half the reason for building the thing, and the specification now says so (Constellation v2.4.117)** — a shadow runner lands: it reads the same intake the turn-spawned board worker reads, drives the same delegation through a resident seat, and writes a *proposed* patch to a separate file. It never touches the board. What keeps it from touching the board is not an instruction but an absence — the shadow seat is given read tools only, so a charter telling it to write has nothing to write with. That is the control this specification names for the layers a wrapper cannot close, applied to its own dogfood.

Previously: v2.6.45 (2026-07-29) — **Two doors, because a rule about who authored the text should be a type and not a discipline (Constellation v2.4.116)** — the resident-seat driver lands, implementing the five operations the specification asks an adapter to provide. The design decision that matters is the submission surface: there are two entry points, one for board-authored content that cannot reach the harness except through the guard, and one for adapter-authored procedure commands that may legitimately be commands. Board content has no path to the second. A single entry point with a flag would encode the same rule as a convention, and a convention is one refactor away from defaulting the wrong way.

Previously: v2.6.44 (2026-07-29) — **A comparison harness that lets the two sides receive different bytes reports an agreement it never tested (Constellation v2.4.115)** — the submission guard now exists twice, because the layer that drives a resident seat follows the harness SDK while the reference runtime does not. Shelling out per submission would put a second runtime in the hot path; maintaining two copies by review is the failure this repository has already shipped three times. So the copies are bound by a **conformance corpus run against both**, with shared constants read from the one measurement record and character classes written explicitly — the shorthand classes differ in Unicode coverage between the two languages, which yields two implementations that hold the same regex and answer differently.

Previously: v2.6.43 (2026-07-29) — **Injection safety for a resident seat is three layers, only one of which can be closed — and that one closes by position, not by framing (Constellation v2.4.114)** — a seat that stays alive and is spoken into by a board accepts board-authored text on the same channel that accepts commands, and a message body beginning with a command sigil is executed as that command. The consequences were measured on a live seat: one line wiped the entire working context and reset the spend ceiling to zero, and the terminal envelope reported a clean success both times, because from the harness's point of view the command did succeed.

Previously: v2.6.42 (2026-07-28) — **A rule with no mechanism is a comment, and the part that diverged was the part the comment did not name (Ultrasafe v0.2.12)** — the code deciding where the assessment ledger lives existed in three copies: the MCP server, the pre-tool trigger, and the turn-end hook. The directory walk was byte-identical in all three. What diverged was the **precedence chain around it** — the two hooks consulted the host-provided project root first, the MCP server did not and followed whatever directory the call happened to carry. In a layout where one repository sits inside another and both carry the marker directories, that single difference is a split ledger: 28 gate events accumulated in one file while the iteration record sat in the other, and a gate reading either file alone undercounts. The MCP copy carried a comment warning that its anchor rule had to match the hook **literally**, and the trigger copy documented having already suffered and fixed this exact class weeks earlier. The MCP copy never received that fix, and the comment did not name the part that broke.

Previously: v2.6.41 (2026-07-28) — **A seat that outlives its context window has to survive the boundary, and the hook that makes that possible is the gate (Constellation v2.4.113 — §13.35 resident seats)** — a board seat is turn-spawned by default: a supervisor watches a log, starts a harness process for the delegation it finds, and the process exits when the turn ends. It pays the wake path and rebuilds the entire harness preamble on every turn even though almost none of it changed, and it has a structural blind spot — a supervisor judging health by exit code cannot tell a turn that did nothing from a turn that did the right thing. The alternative is a **resident** seat: one live session held open, spoken into by the board. This cut specifies what such a seat must provide, and specifies it thin on purpose, because most of what a resident seat needs is now supplied by the harness vendors themselves — session objects that survive many turns, cancellation, context accounting, compaction, session enumeration. What is not supplied upstream is the harness-agnostic, board-aware part, and that is all §13.35 describes. Both modes stay; resident is an option and a deployment with no resident need is not deficient.

The hardest requirement is the compaction cycle, and it is a gate rather than a feature. A resident seat outlives its window, so compaction is not exceptional but routine, and it has three parts that must **all** be available: materialize what exists only in context before the boundary, trigger the compaction rather than discovering one mid-task, and read the materialized handoff back afterward. **A harness that can compact but cannot fire the pre-compaction hook fails this, and such a seat must not run resident** — it would lose, at every boundary, exactly the procedural detail a summary preserves worst, with no way to notice. That is strictly worse than the turn-spawned shape, where the same detail is re-read from disk by construction. The section also refuses a tempting justification: compaction is **not** a cost lever. The reclaimable portion of a context is typically a minority of what is billed per turn and the fixed floor dominates, so a total can rise across a successful compaction; residency pays for itself by not rebuilding the preamble and by removing the wake path, both independent of compaction. A design that cites compaction savings is citing the wrong number.

The second hard requirement is that board-sourced text is **data and must not be dispatched as a command**. Where a harness accepts command sigils on the same channel as ordinary text, a message body starting with one is executed — measured consequences include a seat losing its whole working context and a spend ceiling resetting to zero, both reported as clean successes because the command did succeed. The companion rule is that a terminal success envelope is not evidence that work happened: a non-dispatching input can return success with zero model turns, so advancing an intake cursor on envelope status alone discards the message. Two smaller invariants come along — one owner per inbox, because last-registrant-wins lets a new session silently take over another's intake and the symptom is an absence rather than an error; and stable seat identity across restarts, because a seat recreated with a fresh identity orphans every message addressed to its predecessor. Liveness gets its own clause for the same reason: enumeration proves a process exists, not that it can work, and a probe that never invokes the model will call an exhausted seat healthy.

One deliberate omission is the point of the section. **Per-harness support is not tabulated here** — it is volatile, it belongs in the registry that carries its own `asOf` and revisit cadence, and a capability table written into a specification is a table that will be wrong (the registry has been observed drifting from measurement within two days of being written). Specifications carry the contract; registries carry the current answer. For the same reason no token constants appear in §13.35: the decomposed context reading is a runtime call, and a policy keyed to "the reclaimable share of the gap between floor and threshold" calibrates itself. The section closes by declaring that it is expected to **shrink** — every surface a vendor absorbs should delete text here rather than grow a parallel implementation beside it, and an adapter layer that outgrows this boundary is signalling removal rather than documentation.

Previously: v2.6.40 (2026-07-28) — **A discipline whose tool is gone is a comment (public-document redaction now has a check that can fail)** — this repository holds a rule that public documents carry technical content only: no quoted conversation, and no wording that points at where an internal decision came from. The rule was written down, and a full pass over the repository was executed nine days before this cut. What never existed was anything that could **fail**. So a release entry shipped carrying a phrase of exactly the forbidden class, in four places at once (three README registers plus the changelog row), and it survived until a routine record sweep happened to read that one sentence aloud. A rule that nothing enforces is not a weaker rule; it is a comment, and the only difference from having no rule is that the document says otherwise. This cut removes the phrase and adds the missing check.

Two things about the check are the actual content here. **It discovers by reading every text file, not by an extension allowlist.** The first version used an allowlist and therefore could not see the real violations that were sitting in three shell scripts and a `.gitattributes` file — a finder that cannot see its own targets reports a clean result, which is worse than reporting nothing. Widening the scan moved the measured count from 51 to 55, and those four had been invisible the whole time. **And the two forbidden classes are not treated alike.** Private project identifiers are zero-tolerance, so all twelve occurrences across six files were substituted to role names in this same cut — current file contents only, with ports, protocol strings, lockfile shapes and the surrounding narrative preserved, because rewriting repository history would break every tag and every adopter pin. Eight of those twelve were lower-case spellings that a case-sensitive sweep had already declared clean once. The remaining historical decision-source phrasing is carried as an explicit ceiling that is printed on every run and can only fall; a baseline that rises would mean the ceiling moved instead of the text, so the check says so in its own comment.

One distinction is load-bearing and easy to get wrong: template text stating a principle that applies symmetrically to any adopter is **not** a violation, and the check must not flag it. The seed prompts say an agent records lessons without being told to — that is a generic principle, not a trace of who decided anything. Nine mutation fixtures assert that each forbidden class is genuinely caught and each permitted phrasing genuinely passes, because a check that has never failed is indistinguishable from a check that does not run.

Previously: v2.6.39 (2026-07-28) — **Three critical findings, and all three were about what was running at that moment (Ultrasafe iteration 2 · Constellation v2.4.112 · Ultrasafe v0.2.11)** — The pre-release fan-out ran end to end for the first time: 7 attackers in parallel over 8 axes against the just-cut commit, 50 new findings, and a retest of all 52 from iteration 1. The result that matters is not the count. **The WebSocket upgrade never read `Origin`**, and because a keyless connection is the dashboard by construction, any page the operator had open could open a socket and receive the roster and full history with no HELLO — and therefore no key check, since `requireKey` is evaluated inside a HELLO such a connection never sends. From there: key issuance, enumeration, revocation, SetMain, history deletion, prompt injection into the main agent. Three axes found it independently. The gate now asks whether a **browser** sent the handshake rather than where the client is, because browsers attach `Origin` and cannot be talked out of it while every agent and bridge attaches nothing; inverting that pair would have disconnected every honest client. Separately, **the headless board worker held `Bash(node *)` with no approval step**, which prefix-matches `node -e` and is therefore arbitrary execution, in a working directory holding three board keys in plaintext — one injected delegation from arbitrary code to credential exfiltration, with a 5-second poll and no human anywhere in the path. Narrowing the file tools would have been theater while `node -e` lived, so Bash was removed entirely and the worker's charter rewritten around the tools it kept. Both fixed and the board restarted the same day, with the cross-origin gate measured live on the running instance: agents pass, same-origin passes, cross-origin is destroyed with the reason logged. **The denominator shipped one cut earlier paid for itself on its first real run**: coverage measured **43.6%** against the declared thirteen axes where the old count-what-was-reported arithmetic would have read **70.9%** — the difference between "not yet half covered" and "nearly at the floor" — with the five axes nobody ran returned by name rather than omitted. Of the 52 retests, 10 are fixed and 26 persist, including a key-logging call site that grew from two to three. Two things surfaced that were not in any finding. Removing the worker's Bash traded a security hole for a counting error — its very next turn hand-counted an array and reported 208 against an actual 245, which is exactly what the charter's machine-count rule existed to prevent — so the supervisor now counts and hands the numbers to the worker, since a discipline without a tool is not a discipline. And the fan-out dispossessed its own parent: each sub-agent followed the project's boot ritual, one registered as `role=main`, and inbox ownership resolves to the last registrant — so the real main session's probes began reporting SKIP, which reads as "nothing arrived". Registration now refuses to take an inbox another session holds. That was caught only because the pre-send probe had been mechanized into the push path hours earlier; before that it would have been silence.

Previously: v2.6.38 (2026-07-28) — **A gate that gets easier the less you tell it is indistinguishable from no gate (Ultrasafe v0.2.10)** — The pre-release security fan-out has a coverage condition: what fraction of each axis's applicable attack catalog was actually explored. The spec defines that per axis and never said how the per-axis numbers combine into the one figure the gate compares against its floor, and the omission turned out to be load-bearing. The implementation averaged over **whichever axes the caller chose to report**, so an axis nobody ran contributed nothing rather than zero. Measured on the shipped code: four axes reported, two at 90% and two at 10%, floor 50% — deleting the two weak entries from the report moves the gate from **50% to 90%** without running a single additional probe. The denominator is now the declared axis set (§3.1's thirteen, mirrored machine-readably at `lib/axes.cjs`), an unreported axis scores zero and is returned **by name**, and an id outside the registry is refused rather than averaged in. Two further things were decoration: `applicable_subset_size` and `untested_classes`, which §6.3 calls mandatory, were accepted and echoed back without any predicate reading them — a required field nothing checks is identical to an absent one, so they are now required and their absence yields "could not measure", which is a third state distinct from "measured and below floor". Collapsing those two into one `false` erases why the gate did not open. The prerequisite this unblocks was itself wrong in kind: the approved fan-out was waiting on "write three more attacker skills", a number derived from treating coverage as an axis count, which §6.3 forbids in two places — attacker count and catalog-exploration depth are different quantities, and the fan-out was never actually blocked, since v0.2.x is advisory and cannot block a publish. Also corrected: one attacker skill claimed three axis ids absent from §3.1, so its findings could never have counted; they are catalog classes within `usf-iam-config`, which is what `untested_classes[axis]` is for. The new checker re-derives the 50%→90% figure against a reverted copy on every run, so the claim that this hole is closed can itself fail — and it did, once: the first version of that test reported every axis at the same value, where dropping axes changes nothing, and the self-check caught the test rather than the code.

Previously: v2.6.37 (2026-07-28) — **A gate whose only two outputs are "nothing" and "everything" gets tuned toward "nothing" (Hyperbrief v0.8.0)** — Hyperbrief's routing had exactly two outputs: below the escalation threshold a one-line post-notify, at or above it a nine-section brief. Nothing lay between them, and the threshold is calibrated to keep the expensive output rare — so in practice nearly every decision landed on the one-line side, and a module installed to stop terse decision-dumping went on producing it. The report came from running it across several projects, and the diagnosis is structural rather than incidental: a gate whose only settings are *nothing* and *everything* gets tuned toward *nothing*, because that is the side whose cost is visible. The fix is not a lower threshold, which would trade under-briefing for alert fatigue along the same two-valued axis. It is a third output plus a configurable **floor** — `off` is the prior behavior, `full` briefs every decision point in nine sections, and the new default `summary` guarantees that every decision reaching the gate arrives with an option set. Resolution is a **max over a lattice** (`BLOCKED_STUB < SUMMARY_BRIEF < FULL_HYPERBRIEF`), never an assignment: no setting can lower the tier the rubric demands, so an irreversible cross-module decision still gets nine sections at every setting. That is what makes the default change a behavior change without being a safety change, and migrating back is one line of config. The summary tier's design question was not "what fits" but **which properties survive compression** — a summary that drops the debiasing machinery is not a small brief, it is the nudge this module exists to block. So the schema requires, and the checker verifies by removing each one and demanding rejection: at least two options each carrying **both** a gain and a loss, the cost of doing nothing, the four-branch meta-branch that preserves the reader's right to refuse the question, at least one stated unknown, a `switch_if` that keeps the recommendation conditional rather than a request for consent, and a mandatory escalation affordance. What a summary may omit is **evidence** — the MCDA table, the pre-mortem, the confidence intervals, the decision tree — and only evidence, which is exactly why the one-action path back to that evidence is required rather than recommended: compression without a route to what was compressed is an authority claim. That path is the mirror of the existing tone-floor escape hatch — same mechanism, opposite direction — and the escalated brief reuses the same `decision_id`, because the reader asked one question once and an escalation supersedes nothing. The toggle's only mechanical enforcement point is the PreToolUse hook, which now resolves the floor and names it instead of offering a full brief; an unreadable config resolves to the default **and says so**, since a silent coercion is indistinguishable from the setting having been honored. Two defects surfaced while wiring it. The HTML renderer inlined the IR through a string replacement, so a `$&` anywhere inside a brief silently rewrote the payload — fixed. And the two Markdown templates use block helpers (`{{#if}}`, `{{#each}}`) that the engine never implemented, so every conditional body renders unconditionally and every loop renders once with empty values: a real FullBrief measures **90 empty table cells**. That one is measured and registered rather than fixed here, because implementing the helper subset changes the rendered output of every brief and deserves its own cut; the new summary templates carry zero block syntax and a checker axis enforces it.

Previously: v2.6.36 (2026-07-28) — **A blocker deferred three times for a want of tooling, closed by installing the tool for ten minutes** — The shipped logo and banner had sat un-optimized behind a blocked queue item whose reason was re-verified three times and read the same each time: no lossless PNG optimizer is present. That is a true measurement and it was the wrong conclusion to draw from it, because the tool in question does not need to live in the project — it is a one-shot, and the blocker was really "nobody decided whether to install something", which is an operator call that three re-verifications never surfaced as one. Asked directly, the answer took a sentence. Results: the logo **851,559 → 658,055 bytes (-22.7%)** and the banner **346,315 → 189,854 (-45.2%)**, together 350KB off a public repository's most-fetched files. Two disciplines are the actual content. **"Lossless" is a property of a run, not a word in a tool's README** — the optimizer's guarantee and the assertion that *this* invocation preserved *these* pixels are different claims, so both files are decoded before and after and their raw RGBA buffers compared byte-for-byte, with a mismatch refusing to replace the original rather than reporting a saving. **Metadata is not free to strip**: the obvious next flag removes ICC profiles, which leaves pixels identical while changing how they render, so it is not lossless in the sense that matters for a logo. It was measured rather than argued — stripping saves **16 bytes per file**, so the conservative choice costs nothing and the aggressive one was never a trade. Also asserted: an optimizer that does not shrink a file must leave it alone, since "optimized" and "smaller" are different outcomes and only one of them is worth a write. The procedure is promoted out of scratch space into `scripts/recompress-png-lossless.cjs` — a session-local script is one a later session has to re-derive, and this one carries the pixel check, the metadata reasoning, and the two-line throwaway install in its header. Re-running it now reports **-0.0% on both files**, which is the idempotence check; with the tools absent it prints SKIP and exits 0, because an unobservable check must not count as a passing one. The originals need no separate backup: they are in this repository's history, which is what history is for.

Previously: v2.6.35 (2026-07-28) — **A field whose name ends in `_md` demanded an array, and the name is what an implementer trusts (Hyperbrief v0.7.7 · Ultrasafe v0.2.9)** — The tone-floor escape hatch — the phrases a reader can say to get a brief re-rendered in plain language — is carried in a field named `trigger_phrases_md`. The `_md` suffix reads as *a markdown blob goes here*. The schema demanded an array of strings. Nobody opens a schema to check a field whose name already told them what it holds, so the name wins, and it won **inside this repository**: the Ultrasafe module's escalation payload shipped that field set to an empty string. The array-only form made a value in our own shipped code invalid, and nothing caught it because that payload is assembled rather than validated. Renaming the field is the honest fix and also the one that breaks every existing consumer, including adopters', so the schema now accepts **either form** — which makes the already-shipped code valid rather than retroactively wrong, and stops the name from lying without asking anyone to migrate. Widening a type is where the second defect usually enters: if each consumer interprets the two forms itself, N readers produce N phrase lists, and the symptom — *the plain-language escape hatch does not respond on one particular surface* — is silent on every other one, which is the same failure the streaming-body field produced two cuts ago. So normalization is defined **once**, exported from the renderer engine: a string yields its markdown list items when any are present and its non-empty lines otherwise; an array is trimmed and cleared of blanks; and non-string elements are **dropped rather than coerced**, because a stringified number is a phrase that can never match and would sit in the list looking like coverage. An empty result means *no phrases* and never *the default list*: the default stays declared in the schema and uncopied, since a copied default is the next thing to drift. The empty string was also a functional gap independent of its type — an empty phrase list is an escape hatch that answers nothing — so the affected payload omits the field entirely and inherits the declared default. The checker grew to 24 assertions, one of which had to be taught that **a comment is not code**: its first form matched the quoted `""` inside the comment explaining the fix, reading the record of the repair as evidence the repair had not happened. This decision was routed to the operator rather than taken here, because renaming versus widening is a compatibility call with adopters on the other side of it, and the widening shipped only after that answer came back.

Previously: v2.6.34 (2026-07-28) — **A drift detector reporting drift from another axis is worse than one reporting nothing (Hyperbrief v0.7.6)** — The tone estimator compares a brief's *declared* readability against its *rendered* surface and warns when they diverge by two levels. Its abbreviation axis was estimated from `avg_sentence_length_chars` — so it never looked at an abbreviation. **The defect originated in the specification, not the renderer**: the spec text and the schema description both named average sentence length as this axis's input, and the renderer implemented precisely what was specified. Reading the code alone would have found a faithful implementation. It failed in two directions at once. A text of short plain sentences carrying no acronyms at all estimated **5** — "uses abbreviations bare" — which is the loudest possible warning on the plainest possible surface. And two texts with *identical* abbreviation content estimated 5 and 1 purely because one used longer sentences: measured here at 19 versus 397 characters per sentence, a four-level swing driven entirely by a property the axis does not name. A detector whose warnings are uncorrelated with what a reader would struggle with is worse than silence, because the warning is legible, plausible, and about something else — the operator reconciles a gap that was never there and stops trusting the ones that are. The axis is now driven by **the share of distinct abbreviations left bare at first occurrence**, damped by absolute density so a single stray acronym in a long plain document does not read as a dense surface. Later expansions do not count: the axis is about what a reader meeting the term for the first time was given, which is also why the count is over *distinct* abbreviations rather than occurrences. Sentence length stays in `raw_metrics` — it is a real property worth calibrating against, it simply is not this axis. This is a fix that *removes* a metric from a decision, not from the record. **A non-measurable axis is `null`, and `null` is never a gap.** A brief with no abbreviations has not expanded them all; there was nothing to expand. The axis reports `null` (schema widened to `anyOf` level-or-null, with out-of-range integers still rejected so the widening did not become permission), and the gap comparison skips it rather than coercing it to 0 — which would have manufactured a two-level gap out of an absent measurement, firing the warning precisely where the surface is plainest. The same guard is verified not to have switched the warning off wholesale: a real two-level gap still fires. `first_use_gloss_present` is renamed `gloss_pattern_present`, because it tested whether the text contained any parenthetical at all, related to an abbreviation or not — the name asserted a measurement nobody had implemented, and abbreviation-specific glossing now has its own field. Also removed: the marketplace entry's description pinned `Hyperbrief.md v0.6.0` in prose and had already drifted three minor versions behind, so the pin is gone rather than corrected — one fewer surface that can be wrong. `scripts/verify-hyperbrief-tone-axes.cjs`, 14 assertions, with two doing the load-bearing work: identical-abbreviation texts at 19 and 397 characters per sentence must score **identically**, and the pre-fix formula must **actually disagree** with the new one on those inputs. The second is what keeps the first honest — reverting the fix makes the two agree, and the check fails rather than passing on a tautology.

Previously: v2.6.33 (2026-07-28) — **A refusal that arrives after the payload does not read as a refusal (Constellation v2.4.111)** — An adoption report on the collab reference client carried three defects measured while replacing a hand-rolled client, and the third was a hole in a fix this repository had already recorded as closed. **The board was handed over before the refusal.** §13.25.11 pins each key to the first identity that used it, but the pin is a comparison against a *claimed* identity and the claim exists only in HELLO — so unlike key expiry, that refusal could not be moved ahead of the payload the way §13.25.13 requires. A holder of a leaked collab key received the roster and the full active-channel history, and only then was disconnected. It surfaced because an adopter's read-only tooling did exactly this by accident: the tool returned complete results on every run while the board accumulated refusal notices nobody had connected to it. A refusal that arrives after the payload reads as a working tool plus unexplained noise, which is why the configuration survived long enough to be reported instead of failing on first use. Now a **key-presenting connection receives `AgentList` and `History` only after HELLO clears identity pinning, key requirement, address allowlisting and local-key locality**; `SERVER_HELLO` still goes out immediately, because clients wait on it before sending HELLO and withholding it would deadlock the handshake. **The test is key presence, not network address.** The first draft exempted loopback, and that rule fails in both directions inside a single deployment: behind a reverse proxy every remote client presents as loopback, so the exemption hands the board to exactly the population it was written to exclude; and on a LAN-exposed board the operator's dashboard presents as remote, so it would receive `SERVER_HELLO` and nothing else and come up blank. An adopter running the second arrangement said so before this shipped. Keyless surfaces are unchanged, and the operator address allowlist is explicitly **not** promoted to an identity determination — that would repeat the defect being fixed, treating "passed something" as "is known". The residual class is stated rather than papered over: a connection that sends no HELLO cannot be identified, and retiring the class by giving the board surface its own HELLO is a separate cut with a compatibility window. `ConnectionRejected` now carries `priorStateDelivered`, so a client that keyed itself through the HELLO body — invisible at connection time, so nothing can be withheld from it — can discard what it already took. A per-connection guard keeps the two delivery sites exclusive, because §7 adopters replay `History` into their own stores and a double send multiplies them. And the pin-violation hint stops recommending a separate key per worker: read-only tooling is a second window onto one identity, not a second identity, and issuing it a key puts a phantom in the roster and another credential in the revocation surface — the discipline §6 states for sending, reuse the resident connection rather than opening another, applies unchanged to reading. **The cursor was the one state file that could not be moved.** `COLLAB_STORE`, `COLLAB_OUTBOX` and `COLLAB_UNDELIVERED` are all env-overridable; the outbox cursor was derived from the directory and the agent id, so an adopter running one identity on two boards could separate every file except the one that decides what has already been sent. It now derives from the outbox path — but changing a default path resets a cursor to zero and **re-sends the entire outbox**, the most expensive failure in that file, so the old path is read once and inherited with a line on stderr saying so. The deeper defect is that the §4 lock is keyed on (agent id × board) and therefore *presumes* one identity on two boards, while the state files were not, so the recommended configuration silently shared a send queue and one board's client drained the other's messages — the recipient changed with no signal at all. That arrangement is now **refused at startup** with a named escape rather than absorbed. **"Never visible" and "disappeared" were the same message.** The orphan guard asks the spawner to pass its own pid, because a shell pipeline makes a healthy child look parentless and the guard must not infer. On Git Bash the documented `PARENT_PID=$$` is an MSYS pid rather than a Windows one, so the guard killed healthy sessions on its first tick and phrased it as the parent having vanished — leaving no clue that the cause was a pid-space mismatch. A startup check now separates the two events and names the pid space and the `ps -W` WINPID column, and the configuration that removes the guard's reason to exist — let the harness own the process, since the client reconnects itself and the watching shell is what orphans in the first place — is promoted to the recommended form. **An ambient setting outranked an explicit one, and the destination changed without a signal.** `CONSTELLATION_WS_URL` won unconditionally over `COLLAB_HOST`, so a caller that named a board by argument lost to whatever was in the environment. This repository's own isolated smoke was connecting to the **production board** — its lock file named the wrong board and its test messages landed there — and the visible symptom was ten unrelated-looking assertion failures. Disagreeing board designations are now refused with both values named. The smoke was the second half of the defect: it asserted the lock filename had the right *shape* rather than the right *value*, so a client attached to the wrong board passed that assertion. It now compares against the exact expected name, and both checkers strip ambient designation variables their children were not explicitly given, because a test that does not control its own inputs is testing something else. The first attempt at that fix is the more useful half of the lesson: stripping the ambient variable dropped the client to its *default* board — the production hub this machine hosts — so the flood moved rather than stopped, and it was a counterparty on that board who noticed. **Removing contamination is not the same as specifying a destination.** Test clients are now pinned to a port nothing listens on, and each run parses the address the client announces and aborts the whole check if it is not a test board, which caught a second ordering mistake in the same function immediately: the strip loop was deleting the destination the runner had just set. §13.13.3 gains **(h)**: the streaming body rides in a top-level `delta`, not `value`, so a consumer written from the `name`+`value` carrier shape emits blank records without erroring. `scripts/verify-collab-contracts.cjs` — 23 assertions against a real server booted on an isolated tree, plus a **per-run mutation check** that boots a second copy with the gate reverted and requires those assertions to fail there, so "this checker can fail" is a property of every run rather than a claim made once. The reverted copy reproduces the reported order exactly: `SERVER_HELLO, AgentList, History, ConnectionRejected`.

Previously: v2.6.32 (2026-07-27) — **A partial fix reads exactly like a fix, so a retest found the half that was left (Constellation v2.4.110)** — Before acting on a security-gate decision, the 10 critical/high findings from Ultrasafe iteration 1 were retested against HEAD by three scoped delegations with one rule that did the work: **`FIXED` requires pointing at the code that fixes it** — "I could not find the vulnerable pattern" is `CANNOT_DETERMINE`, because absence of evidence is not evidence. Eight were genuinely closed with code and checker citations. Two were not, and both were the same shape — a fix applied to one of the paths it needed to cover. **crypto-04**: `wsValidKey` had been converted to `timingSafeEqual`, while `wsKeyRole` and `keyFind` still compared with `===` — and those two are the *only* gate on `/join/collab|upstream|peer` and decide role and privilege on both the upgrade and the HELLO path. Both are now early-exit-free constant-time scans that keep iterating after a match, since stopping at the match leaks which position differed. The two remaining direct comparisons are pinned in an **allowlist with stated reasons** (revocation, where the operator already holds the key; relabelling, where both sides are server-held) so a new direct comparison anywhere fails the count. **se-05**: `maybePush` had no limit of any kind, so any qualifying `CUSTOM` rang a device — one reconnect loop would have been enough, and this repository has measured an `AgentHello` flood. Worse, the notification *title* derived its identity from `msg.agentName`, which the server re-stamped for `agentId` and `source` but not for the display name, so the title was forgeable. Now: a per-sender cooldown plus an independent global per-minute cap, with **suppression counted and carried into the next notification body** — a suppressed count of zero and a limiter that is not running must not look alike. And the server re-stamps `agentName` from the authenticated connection, **deleting the field** when there is no authenticated name, because absent is more accurate than forged; that fixes provenance for every consumer, not just push. The limiter is exposed as a seam taking the clock as an argument, so 7 assertions verify it deterministically (first passes, immediate resend blocked, a different sender passes, post-cooldown passes, the global cap is independent, suppression is counted, reset isolates) rather than a test that actually waits ten seconds and flakes under load. `smoke-auth-binding` 30 → 49 assertions. All three delegations also checked the **derived copies** — the live board `server.cjs` and the installed plugin cache — because "fixed at the source, still vulnerable as deployed" is a failure this repository has measured. The retest is deliberately **not** recorded as iteration 2: a 3-axis retest is not comparable to a 7-axis pass, and filing it would record `coverage` as 3/13 = 0.23, making a metric decline when nothing declined. That exposed a gap left open rather than papered over — **there is no writer for a retest record**; `ultrasafe_iteration_record` accepts only iterations, which is the same shape as the `meth-03` finding ("no writer existed, so the counter never moved"). Evidence: `reports/2026-07-27-ultrasafe-retest-3axis.md`.

Previously: v2.6.31 (2026-07-27) — **The release gate's coverage floor was written three times with three different values, and one of the units made it always pass (Ultrasafe v0.2.8)** — The clean-signal gate's coverage condition was implemented twice, independently. The Stop-hook evaluator — the surface that runs on every tool call and wrote the recorded gate state — hardcoded **0.85**, tier-blind, a number the spec states for no tier. The MCP `ultrasafe_clean_signal_check` used the spec's per-Tier table (**50 / 75 / 90**). A brief written the day before cited a third figure, 0.8.
